\chapter*{Preface}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface}

this document introduces a RISC architecture designed with simplicity
as its dominating principle, along with several features not yet present
in mainstream RISC architectures:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
  \item a compression technique for split instruction and constant streams.
  \item a variable-length instruction format designed for vectorized decoding.
  \item an efficient frontier between hardware synthesis and software translation.
  \item a modern approach to virtualization, memory management, and capabilities.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the document begins with a general introduction to RISC architectures,
including principal elements such as load-store operation, memory,
registers, instructions, and constants. it then presents an overview of
the proposed architecture, followed by sections on program streams, the
variable-length instruction format, the instruction set, its assembly
syntax, and the programming language calling convention.
\\
\par\noindent
this document does not simply present an architecture reimplementing
existing ideas; rather, it introduces a novel architecture featuring an
instruction coding scheme optimized for efficient parallel decoding in
hardware or software, combined with contemporary practices in instruction
semantics, including the use of single-bit predicates for compare, divide,
branch, plus arithmetic instructions with carry, to achieve an optimal
mapping to vectors.
\\
\par\noindent
in addition to the core instruction set, the architecture also defines
extensions for capabilities, virtual domains, software-defined memory
management and environment. the capability extension adds fine-grained
page-based permissions and role based access control. the domain
extension adds virtualization for trap routing, page translation, and
context switching. the machine extension provides for custom address
translation schemes. finally, the environment extension adds controls
for power and reset.
\\
\par\noindent
this work is intended for computer architects, systems programmers,
students of computing machinery, and anyone interested in the evolution
of RISC design. by the end of the document, readers should be able to
reason about computer programs at the instruction level and understand
the logic behind the architectural design choices—with enough detail
to implement the architecture in hardware or software.

\chapter{Architecture}
\pagenumbering{arabic}

\section{Introduction}

this section gives a brief introduction to RISC architectures.
\\
\par\noindent
A RISC\footnote{Reduced Instruction Set Computer} machine is a type of
general-purpose computer with the characteristic that it has a reduced
set of instructions in contrast to a CISC\footnote{Complex Instruction
Set Computer} machine. A RISC machine is Turing complete meaning it can
perform any computation that a Turing machine can, given enough time and
memory. a Turing machine~\cite{Turing1936} is a theoretical model of
computation.
\\
\par\noindent
a RISC machine has a set of instructions which comprise basic operations
such as: \emph{load-from-memory}, \emph{store-to-memory}, \emph{add},
\emph{subtract}, \emph{compare}, plus \emph{conditional branch} and
\emph{unconditional branch} instructions et cetera; which one can imagine
as a list of instructions on a paper tape. each instruction has an
\emph{opcode}, which is a unique binary pattern that identifies the
\emph{operation}, plus several \emph{operands}, which are arguments
to the instruction.

\begin{Verbatim}
    register-0 = load-from-memory at tape-address-0
    register-1 = load-from-memory at tape-address-1
    register-2 = add register-0 and register-1
                 store-to-memory register-2 at tape-address-2 
\end{Verbatim}

\par\noindent
some instructions have operands that point to values inside of
\emph{registers} in a \emph{register-file} which is like a close filing
cabinet containing cards with numbers on them, and some of these numbers
are addresses that point to values in \emph{main-memory} which is like a
larger but slower filing cabinet. some of these values are \emph{immediate}
values which are small numbers listed inside of the instructions on the
paper tape.
\\
\par\noindent
the paper tape is just a way conceptualize a list of instructions stored
in \emph{main-memory}. there is a special register called \emph{PC} short
for \emph{program counter}, which points to the current position on the
tape. after each instruction executes the tape is advanced to the next
instruction and the \emph{program counter} is incremented, until it
encounters a \emph{branch} instruction which causes it to move forwards
or backwards to a different position on the tape. branch instructions can
be \emph{conditional} or \emph{unconditional}. conditional branches are
selectively executed based on the results of a comparison instruction.

\newpage
\subsection{load-store}

a \emph{load-store} architecture~\cite{cocke1990risc} is a way to
characterize RISC architectures where most instructions have simple operands
that point to values held in registers, plus \emph{load} and \emph{store}
instructions to retrieve and commit values to main memory. a \emph{load-store}
architecture alleviates the need to add complex addressing modes,
plus intput-output to peripherals and secondary storage use
\emph{MMIO}\footnote{\emph{MMIO}\,-- Memory-mapped I/O.} to avoid needing
special \emph{Input/Output} instructions.

\subsection{registers}

registers are temporary storage used to fill input and output
operands for the \emph{ALU}\footnote{\emph{ALU}\,-- Arithmetic logic unit.}
before and after execution of instructions. registers are
organized as a word-addressable store where each register number refers
to $XLEN \in \{64, 128\}$ bits of data. \emph{XLEN} is a parameter that
specifiess the width of registers in bits.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \fill [black!5] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black!95] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\z}{int(3-\x)}
  \node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (4,\x+0.25)  {\textsf{register \z}};
}

\draw [black!95,dashed] (0,-1)      rectangle (8,0);

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black!95] (8,\x)    rectangle (24,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \foreach \y in {0,4,...,60} {
    \fill [black!5]  (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
    \draw [black!45] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
  }
  \foreach \y in {2,6,...,62} {
    \fill [black!9] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
    \draw [black!45] (8+\y/4,\x)    rectangle (8.5+\y/4,\x+1);
  }
  \draw [black!95] (8,\x) rectangle (24,\x+1);
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (8,4.2)  {\textsf{XLEN}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (24,4.2)  {\textsf{0}};

\draw [black!95,dashed] (8,-1)      rectangle (24,0);

\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{organization of register storage.}
\label{fig:register-organization}
\end{figure}

\subsection{memory}

main memory is primary storage which in modern computers is most likely
\emph{DRAM}\footnote{\emph{DRAM}\,-- Dynamic random-access memory.}.
main memory is organized as a byte-addressable store where each address
refers to a byte which is 8-bits of data. \emph{ALEN} is a parameter that
specifies the width of addresses in bits.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]

\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \fill [black!5] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3}
  \draw [black!95] (0,\x)    rectangle (8,\x+1);
\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\z}{int((3-\x)*8)}
  \node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (4,\x+0.25)  {\textsf{address \z}};
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,4.2)  {\textsf{ALEN}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (8,4.2)  {\textsf{0}};

\draw [black!95,dashed] (0,-1)      rectangle (8,0);

\foreach \x in {0,...,3} {
  \foreach \y in {0,2,...,6} {
    \fill [black!5] (8+\y*2,\x)     rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
    \draw [black!95] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
  }
  \foreach \y in {1,3,...,7} {
    \fill [black!9] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
    \draw [black!95] (8+\y*2,\x)    rectangle (10+\y*2,\x+1);
  }
}
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (9,4.2)  {\textsf{byte-7}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (23,4.2)  {\textsf{byte-0}};

\draw [black!95,dashed] (8,-1)      rectangle (24,0);

\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{organization of main-memory storage.}
\label{fig:main-memory-organization}
\end{figure}

\newpage
\subsection{instructions}
\label{sec:instructions}

instruction memory is a memory region dedicated to program instructions.
it is organized as a word-addressable store, similar to main memory,
but is read-only and reserved exclusively for instructions. this
configuration is typically referred to as a Harvard architecture%
~\cite{aiken1946marki}, in contrast to a Von Neumann architecture%
~\cite{vonneumann1945edvac}, which uses a shared memory region for
both program instructions and data.
\\
\par\noindent
instruction words contain packets of 16-bits which are composed of
size, opcode, and operand fields. instruction packets can be appended
together to form larger instruction words with larger opcode and
operand fields. section~\ref{sec:instruction-format} provides details
on the instruction template and instruction forms.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\node at (15.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {1};
\node at (16.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {0};
\node at (10.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {6};
\node at (14.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {2};
\node at (1.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {15};
\node at (9.500000,1.250000) [font=\scriptsize] {7};
\draw (15.000000,1.000000) -- (15.000000,1.500000);
\draw (10.000000,1.000000) -- (10.000000,1.500000);
\draw (1.000000,1.000000) -- (1.000000,1.500000);
\draw (17.000000,1.000000) -- (17.000000,1.500000);

\par\noindent
instruction words are packed together into instruction blocks
containing instruction size information that is used to fuse
them together to compose variable-length instructions~\cite{US5717881A}.
instruction words can begin on any 16-bit aligned address.
instruction memory is addressed with displacements from the \emph{program
counter} called \emph{pc-relative} addresses.
\\

\newpage
\subsection{constants}
\label{sec:constants}

constant memory~\cite{Solomonoff1964} is a memory region dedicated to
constants. it is organized as a word-addressable store like main-memory,
but is read-only and restricted to constants. instructions can refer to
constants using \emph{ib-relative} addresses inside instruction operands.
section~\ref{sec:program-streams} provides more details on the split
instruction and constant streams.
\\
\par\noindent
constant memory is addressed with displacements from the \emph{immediate
base} register called \emph{ib-relative} addresses which are stored inside
instruction operand slots, are sized, scaled and aligned addresses computed
relative to the \emph{immediate base} register, which is like a program
counter for constants. these diagrams show $ib8(n)$ through $ib64(n)$
\emph{ib-relative} addresses for access to 8-bit through to 64-bit constants
respectively: \emph{note: ib-relative addresses alias a single constant
storage space containing all types.}
\\

\newpage
\section{Program streams}
\label{sec:program-streams}

glyph separates the stored programs into two streams, one with instructions
and one with constants~\cite{Solomonoff1964}. the instruction stream is
addressed with the \emph{program counter (pc)} and the constant stream is
addressed with the \emph{immediate base (ib)} register. this could be
referred to as split program stream Harvard architecture.

\caption{program counter and immediate base register.}
\label{fig:glyph-program-streams}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
instruction blocks are aligned memory blocks addressed by the \emph{program
counter}. instruction memory is addressed with \emph{pc-relative} addresses
in instruction immediate values or indirectly with constants accessed using
\emph{ib-relative} addresses stored inside instruction operand slots.
\\
\par\noindent
immediate blocks are aligned memory blocks addressed by the \emph{immediate
base} register. constant memory is addressed with \emph{ib-relative}
addresses stored inside instruction operand slots, which are sized, scaled,
and aligned relative to the \emph{immediate base} register.
\\
\par\noindent
the constant stream branches independently using the
\emph{immediate-block-jump} instruction to update the \emph{immediate base}
register, or together with the \emph{program counter} in procedure call and
return instructions that branch instruction and constants at the same time;
\emph{jump-and-link-immediate-block} and \emph{jump-to-link-immediate-block}
add and subtract address vectors to \emph{(pc,ib)}. the \emph{pack-indirect}
instruction allows absolute \emph{(pc,ib)} addresses to be packed into an
address vector for indirect calls.
\\
\par\noindent
the instruction forms use bonded operand slots for immediate operands and
operand bits do not overlap opcode bits. the use of immediate blocks means
large immediate constants can all be accessed using short references encoded
inside of operand slots for instructions that use an immediate block
relative addressing mode.
\\

\newpage
\section{Core concepts}

\par\noindent
glyph is a super regular RISC architecture that encodes constants in a
secondary stream accessed via an \emph{immediate base} register that points at
immediate blocks containing constants accessed via a constant address
mode. the \emph{immediate base} register branches like the
\emph{program counter}, and procedure calls and returns set and restore
\emph{(pc,ib)} together.
\\
\par\noindent
glyph uses relative address vectors in its link register which is different
to typical RISC architectures. glyph does this so that the branch instructions
can fit \emph{(pc,ib)} into the a single link register for compatibility with
traditional RISC architectures. glyph achieves this by packing two relative
\emph{(pc,ib)} displacements into a relative address vector\footnote{the
architecture defines two parameters: \emph{ALEN} and \emph{XLEN},
which respectively to refer to width of addresses and width of general
purpose registers in bits. when $XLEN \ge ALEN \times 2$ it is possible to
pack absolute addresses instead of relative addresses, as would be the case
where \emph{ALEN=64} and \emph{XLEN=128}.}.
\\
\par\noindent
immediate blocks can be switched using the immediate block branch
instruction. immediate blocks, unlike typical RISC architectures, mean
that most relocations are word sized like CISC architectures, and can
use C-style structure packing and alignment rules.
\\
\par\noindent
this list outlines some differentiating elements of the super regular
RISC architecture:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item variable length instruction format supporting 16, 32, and 64-bit
instructions.
\item 1-bit predicate for compare, branch, add with carry, and subtract with
borrow.
\item 16-bit compressed instruction packets that can access 8 registers.
\item \emph{(pc,ib)} is a \emph{program counter} and
\emph{immediate base} register address vector.
\item link register contains a packed relative \emph{(pc,ib)} address vector
to function entry.
\item \texttt{ibj} --- \emph{immediate-block-jump} adds a relative address
to the \emph{immediate base} register.
\item \texttt{mov} --- \emph{move-word-immediate-block} uses a displacement
to access a constant.
\item \texttt{jalib} --- \emph{jump-and-link-immediate-block} or
\emph{link-function-call} copies the address vector into the link register
and adds constants to \emph{(pc,ib)}. it is used to branch the \emph{program
counter} and \emph{immediate base} register at the same time for calling
procedures.
\item \texttt{jtlib} --- \emph{jump-to-link-immediate-block} or
\emph{link-function-return} subtracts link vector from and adds constants
to \emph{(pc,ib)}. it is used to branch the \emph{program counter} and the
\emph{immediate base} register at the same time for returning from called
procedures.
\item \texttt{pin} --- \emph{pack-indirect} packs two absolute addresses as
relative address vector from \emph{(pc,ib)} and is used for calling
absolute addresses such as virtual functions.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the \texttt{pin} and \texttt{link} instructions form a modular arithmetic
ring of relative address vectors, due to their use of relative addresses.
relative address vectors can be encrypted, decrypted, and authenticated to
form a verifiable chain of addresses for control flow integrity.

\newpage
\section{Core parameters}

\par\noindent
glyph has several architectural parameters that define the dimensions
of an instance of the architecture to allow the architecture to be
instantiated in several different configurations. these configurations
are grouped into architectural profiles.

\subsection{architectural parameters}

glyph defines a unified floating-point and integer scalar register file
with 8 or 64 registers and an optional unified floating-point and integer
vector register file with 64 registers.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 16-bit instruction packet can access 8 registers with up to 3 operands.
\item 32-bit instruction packet can access 64 registers with up to 3 operands.
\item 64-bit instruction packet can access 64 registers with up to 6 operands.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
glyph architectural parameters are as follows:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value}& \textbf{Description} \\
\hline
SREG & 8, 64      & number of scalar registers \\
VREG & --, 64     & number of vector registers \\
NDOM & --, 16     & number of domain contexts \\
NCOL & --, 16     & number of architectural colors \\
MLEN & 32         & width of message addresses in bits \\
ILEN & 16, 32, 64 & width of largest instruction in bits \\
ALEN & 64         & width of arithmetic on scalars in bits \\
XLEN & 64, 128    & width of scalar registers in bits \\
VLEN & 512, 4096  & width of vector registers in bits \\
GLEN & 128        & width of vector lane group in bits \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{architectural parameters}
\label{tab:architectural-parameters}
\end{table}

\vspace{-1em}

\subsection{architectural profiles}

glyph architectural parameters are linked to the dimensions of the
register slots in the variable length instruction packets. the 16-bit
packet can be used in a freestanding micro-controller profile. the 32-bit
packet contains scalar and packed-SIMD\footnote{SIMD - Single Instruction
Multiple Data} instructions on scalar registers. the 64-bit packet
contains packed-SIMD instruction on vector registers.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{profile} &
\textbf{SREG} & \textbf{VREG} & \textbf{NDOM} & \textbf{NCOL} & \textbf{MLEN} &
\textbf{ILEN} & \textbf{ALEN} & \textbf{XLEN} & \textbf{VLEN} & \textbf{GLEN} \\
\hline
\emph{scalar-min} & 8  & -  & -  & -  & 32  & 16 & 64  & 64  & -    & -   \\
\emph{scalar-max} & 64 & -  & 16 & 16 & 32  & 32 & 64  & 128 & -    & -   \\
\emph{vector-min} & 64 & 64 & 16 & 16 & 32  & 64 & 64  & 128 & 512  & 128 \\
\emph{vector-max} & 64 & 64 & 16 & 16 & 32  & 64 & 64  & 128 & 4096 & 128 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{architectural profiles}
\label{tab:architectural-profiles}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Instruction format}
\label{sec:instruction-format}

instructions use a variable length format~\cite{US5717881A} with a
16-bit instruction packet that supports 16, 32, 64, and 128-bit
instruction words. the instruction packet uses a \emph{super regular}
scheme, whereby successive instruction packets extend the fields in
the previous instruction packet~\cite{US6877084B1}. instruction words
can begin on any 16-bit aligned address.


\subsection{instruction sizes}

the variable length instruction format has a \emph{2-bit} size field
inspired by LEB128~\cite{leb128} in a fixed position in every instruction
packet, to reduce the complexity of size-decoding for variable length
instructions\footnote{instructions are considered \emph{well-formed}
if the size field of subsequent packets has the value \texttt{11}.}.
this table shows the size fields for the different instruction sizes:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|c|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Instruction size} & \textbf{Size vector} \\
\hline
16-bit   & \texttt{\{00}\}                       \\
32-bit   & \texttt{\{01,11\}}                    \\
64-bit   & \texttt{\{10,11,11,11\}}              \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{variable-length instruction size fields}
\label{tab:instruction-size-fields}
\end{table}

\subsection{instruction decoding}

the instruction size encoding and field extension scheme has been designed
to minimize complexity for parallel decoding in hardware and software.
the following table shows the instruction width combinations for
various width parallel instruction decoders.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
\textbf{Packets} & \textbf{Bits} & \textbf{Bytes} & $\bm{n_{combos}}$ & $\bm{\log_2(n)}$ \\
\hline
1-wide  & 16-bit   & 2  & 1     & \fmtlogtwo{1} \\
2-wide  & 32-bit   & 4  & 16    & \fmtlogtwo{16} \\
4-wide  & 64-bit   & 8  & 58    & \fmtlogtwo{58} \\
8-wide  & 128-bit  & 16 & 574   & \fmtlogtwo{574} \\
16-wide & 256-bit  & 32 & 51904 & \fmtlogtwo{51904} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{instruction decode combinations}
\label{tab:instruction-decode-combos}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{instruction forms}

the variable length instruction format has a single base format
where fields in the template instruction form are extended by
successive instruction packets.
\\
\par\noindent
the instructions forms are super regular in that operand and opcode
bits do not overlap and the number and complexity of the formats is
reduced so that vectorized instruction decoding is simple in both
hardware and software. the scheme is designed so that \emph{1-bit} of
coding space in the larger packet can be used to extend register sizes.

\newpage
\section{Register file}

glyph has register state comprised of a predicate register, program
counter, and immediate base registers, a unified floating-point and integer
scalar register file, and an optional unified floating-point and integer
vector register file, each with an extensible number of registers.

\subsection{template configuration}

the register state accessible by the template architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item ALEN-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item ALEN-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item SREG \texttimes\,XLEN-bit scalar registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r\textit{N}}).
\item VREG \texttimes\,VLEN-bit vector registers (\textsf{v0} --- \textsf{v\textit{N}}).
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the template configuration
register state:
\\

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering

\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\foreach \x in {0,...,7} {
  \ifnum\x=6\relax
    % skip r6
  \else
    \fill [black!5] (8,7-\x)    rectangle (16,8-\x);
  \fi
}
\foreach \x in {0,...,7} {
\ifnum\x=6\relax
    % skip r6
  \else
    \draw [black!95] (8,7-\x)    rectangle (16,8-\x);
  \fi
}

  \node [font=\scriptsize, anchor=base] at (12,{7.25-\x}) {\textsf{r\x}};

    % skip r6

    \fill [black!5] (0,-1-\x)    rectangle (16,-2-\x);

}

  \node [font=\scriptsize, anchor=base] at (8,{-1.75-\x}) {\textsf{v\x}};

\node[black!50, font=\large] at (12,1.70) {$\vdots$};

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{scalar-min} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{scalar-min} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 8 \texttimes\,64-bit scalar registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r7}).
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the \emph{scalar-min} configuration
register state:
\\

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]

\foreach \x in {0,...,7}

\foreach \x in {0,...,7}
  \draw [black!95] (12,\x)    rectangle (16,\x+1);
\foreach \i in {0,...,7}
  \node [font=\scriptsize, anchor=base] at (14,{7.25-\i}) {\textsf{r\i}};

\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (12,11.2)  {\textsf{64}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (16,11.2)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (12,8.2)  {\textsf{64}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (16,8.2)  {\textsf{0}};

\fill [black!15] (12,9)    rectangle (16,10);
\draw [black!95] (12,9)    rectangle (16,10);

\fill [black!15] (12,10)    rectangle (16,11);
\draw [black!95] (12,10)    rectangle (16,11);

\fill [black!15] (14,12)    rectangle (16,13);
\draw [black!95] (14,12)    rectangle (16,13);

\node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (14,10.25)  {\textsf{pc}};
\node [font=\scriptsize,anchor=base] at (15,12.25)  {\textsf{flag}};

\draw [decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt,mirror,raise=2mm}]
    (16,12) -- (16,13) node[midway,xshift=3mm,align=center,font=\scriptsize,
        text width=17mm,anchor=west]{\textsf{\shortstack{predicate}}};

\draw [decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt,mirror,raise=2mm}]

\caption{\emph{scalar-min} configuration register state.}
\label{fig:glyph-register-state-scalar-min}
\end{figure}

\par\noindent
when $XLEN < ALEN \times 2$ it is not possible to pack absolute addresses
in the link register, so the \texttt{PIN} and \texttt{LINK} instructions
save relative addresses in the link register. this increases latency in
branch address calculation compared to absolute addresses.

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{scalar-max} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{scalar-max} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 64 \texttimes\,128-bit scalar registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r63}).
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following diagram shows the \emph{scalar-max} configuration
register state:
\\

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=0.5, yscale=0.45]
\begin{scope}[every node/.style={font=\small, inner sep=0pt, right}]
\foreach \x in {0,...,7} {
  \ifnum\x=6\relax
    % skip r6
  \else
    \fill [black!5] (8,7-\x)    rectangle (16,8-\x);
  \fi
}
\foreach \x in {0,...,7} {

\par\noindent
when $XLEN \ge ALEN \times 2$ it is possible to pack absolute addresses
in the link register, so the \texttt{PIN} and \texttt{LINK} instructions
save absolute addresses in the link register instead. this reduces latency
in branch address calculation compared to relative addresses.

\newpage
\subsection{\emph{vector-min} configuration}

the register state accessible by the \emph{vector-min} architectural
profile is comprised of:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item 1-bit predicate register (\emph{flag}).
\item 64-bit \emph{program counter} register (aligned to 2 bytes).
\item 64-bit \emph{immediate base} register (aligned to 64 bytes).
\item 64 \texttimes\,128-bit scalar registers (\textsf{r0} --- \textsf{r63}).
\item 64 \texttimes\,512-bit vector registers (\textsf{v0} --- \textsf{v63}).
\end{itemize}

\newpage
\section{Type formats}

there are three basic scalar types used with the scalar and packed SIMD instructions:

\vspace{-0.3em}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item unsigned fixed-width integer
\item signed fixed-width two's complement integer
\item fixed-width binary floating-point number
\end{itemize}

\subsection{numeric rules}

this section describes the numeric encoding, arithmetic, and comparison
rules for signed and unsigned integers and for floating-point numbers.

\subsubsection{unsigned fixed-width integer rules}

unsigned fixed-width integers are represented using an \emph{n}-bit
form, with range $[0,\; 2^{n}-1]$. arithmetic operations on unsigned
integers wrap around on overflow according to modulo $2^n$ arithmetic.
relational comparisons are performed according to unsigned magnitude.

\subsubsection{signed fixed-width integer rules}

signed fixed-width integers are represented using an \emph{n}-bit
two’s complement form, with range $[-2^{n-1},\; 2^{n-1}-1]$.
negative numbers are encoded by inverting all bits and adding one.
addition and subtraction operations on signed integers wrap
around on overflow according to modulo $2^n$ arithmetic.
multiplication and division operations on signed integers truncate
toward zero and set the sign to the \emph{exclusive-or} of the two signs.
relational comparisons are performed according to signed magnitude.

\subsubsection{binary floating-point number rules}

binary floating‑point number formats and arithmetic rules defined
in this specification conform to the IEEE standard for floating‑point
arithmetic, \emph{IEEE-754-2019}~\cite{ieee754-2019}, which specifies
fixed‑width binary interchange formats, encoding of sign, exponent,
and fraction fields, and defines arithmetic, comparison, and rounding
behavior, including the handling of exceptional values such as NaNs
and infinities.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item the quiet NaN has the most significant bit of the fraction field set to one.
\item the signaling NaN has the most significant bit of the fraction field set to zero.
\item the canonical NaNs have the sign bit and all remaining payload bits set to zero.
\item all other NaN payloads are reserved for the implementation.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the 8‑bit binary floating‑point formats follow the definitions in the
current IEEE P754 working draft, and the 16‑bit brain floating‑point
format \emph{bfloat16} is specified by the \emph{OpenCL bfloat16
Conversions Extension}.

the following binary floating-point number formats are supported.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering

binary16 & 5 & 10 & IEEE 754 16-bit floating-point \\
binary32 & 8 & 23 & IEEE 754 32-bit floating-point \\
binary64 & 11 & 52 & IEEE 754 64-bit floating-point \\
binary128 & 15 & 112 & IEEE 754 128-bit floating-point \\
\hline
\end{tabular}

\label{tab:ieee754-floating-point-number-formats}
\end{table}

\section{Type conventions}

glyph adopts a common type naming convention for integer and floating-point
scalar and packed SIMD vector types. each type is described by several attributes:

\vspace{-0.3em}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item unique type name
\item unique short symbol used in opcodes
\item vector length (omitted for scalar types)
\item mnemonic indicating the operand type and width
\item human-readable description
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
short symbols for signed integers have single-letter codes. unsigned types
and floating-point symbols are explicitly distinguished by \texttt{u} and
\texttt{f} suffixes, eliminating ambiguity with single-letter symbols.
the mnemonics have been chosen to disambiguate operand size and type.

\subsection{scalar types}

the following table lists scalar types, their symbols, mnemonics and descriptions:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{type} & \textbf{symbol} & \textbf{mnemonic} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{i8}   & \texttt{b}  & \emph{byte}   & 8-bit signed integer (byte) \\
\texttt{i16}  & \texttt{w}  & \emph{word}   & 16-bit signed integer (word) \\
\texttt{i32}  & \texttt{d}  & \emph{dword}  & 32-bit signed integer (double-word) \\
\texttt{i64}  & \texttt{q}  & \emph{qword}  & 64-bit signed integer (quad-word) \\
\texttt{i128} & \texttt{o}  & \emph{oword}  & 128-bit signed integer (octa-word) \\
\texttt{u8}   & \texttt{bu} & \emph{ubyte}  & 8-bit unsigned integer (byte) \\
\texttt{u16}  & \texttt{wu} & \emph{uword}  & 16-bit unsigned integer (word) \\
\texttt{u32}  & \texttt{du} & \emph{udword} & 32-bit unsigned integer (double-word) \\
\texttt{u64}  & \texttt{qu} & \emph{uqword} & 64-bit unsigned integer (quad-word) \\
\texttt{u128} & \texttt{ou} & \emph{uoword} & 128-bit unsigned integer (octa-word) \\
\texttt{f8e4} & \texttt{p4} & - & IEEE 754-draft 8-bit floating-point \emph{p4} (binary8p4) \\
\texttt{f8e5} & \texttt{p5} & - & IEEE 754-draft 8-bit floating-point \emph{p5} (binary8p5) \\
\texttt{bf16} & \texttt{bf} & - & OpenCL 16-bit brain floating-point (bfloat16) \\
\texttt{f16}  & \texttt{hf} & \emph{half}   & IEEE 754 16-bit floating-point (binary16) \\
\texttt{f32}  & \texttt{sf} & \emph{float}  & IEEE 754 32-bit floating-point (binary32) \\
\texttt{f64}  & \texttt{df} & \emph{double} & IEEE 754 64-bit floating-point (binary64) \\
\texttt{f128} & \texttt{qf} & \emph{quad}   & IEEE 754 128-bit floating-point (binary128) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{scalar types}

\end{table}

the following section describes vector types from 128-bit through to 4096-bit.

\foreach \factor/\width/\sym\name/\bits in
 {0/128/xv/xmm/000, 1/256/yv/ymm/001, 2/512/zv/zmm/010,
  3/1024/uv/umm/011, 4/2048/vv/vmm/100, 5/4096/wv/wmm/101} {

\newpage
\section{Memory model}

glyph adopts a simple and pragmatic memory model designed for practical
implementation in hardware or for translation to processors with
weakly ordered \emph{acquire-release} memory models and to processors with
\emph{strongly-ordered} memory models possessing a \emph{total store order}.

\subsection{address spaces}

glyph divides the address space into five categories of memory
with different characteristics:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \emph{user} --- virtual memory accessible to unprivileged software with \emph{user} role.
\item \emph{system} --- virtual memory accessible to privileged software with \emph{system} role.
\item \emph{physical} --- physical memory visible globally with well-defined ordering rules.
\item \emph{group} --- high-speed physical memory visible to a group of shared threads.
\item \emph{private} --- high-speed physical memory visible to only a single thread.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the \emph{group} and \emph{private} address spaces are defined to align
with the OpenCL\footnote{OpenCL -- \emph{The OpenCL Specification} (API,
Platform, and Language Specifications), Khronos Group.} memory model.
the \emph{group} address space corresponds to OpenCL work-group shared
memory (\textsf{\_\_local}), while the \emph{private} address space
corresponds to OpenCL per-thread memory (\textsf{\_\_private}). the
visibility, lifetime, and synchronization semantics of these address
spaces follow the principles of OpenCL work-group and private memory,
including the requirement for explicit synchronization to establish
ordering and visibility between threads accessing \emph{group} memory.

\subsection{atomic operations}

glyph is designed to support translation to instruction sets with
\emph{weakly-ordered} memory operations. to achieve this, all
\emph{strongly-ordered} operations with writes must have
implicit memory ordering barriers with respect to relaxed memory
operations. this means any instruction with \emph{strongly-ordered}
writes must be preceded by an implicit memory barrier such that
prior writes may not be reordered past a \emph{strongly-ordered} or
\emph{synchronized} memory write, subsequent reads may not be reordered
before such a write, and reads to the same memory location preserve
program order. i.e. \emph{write–write}, \emph{write–read}, and
\emph{read–read} ordering constraints.
\\
\par\noindent
translation to \emph{weakly-ordered} memory systems with \emph{acquire-release}
ordering requires implicit barriers around locked atomic operations because
they act as implicit synchronization points. \emph{acquire} loads are
relaxed except for ordering enforced by prior \emph{strongly-ordered}
stores, and constrain only subsequent memory operations. the addition
of scoped \emph{group} and \emph{private} address spaces ameliorates the
cost of maintaining a total store order on global memory.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \emph{group} --- shared memory with visibility scoped to a localized group of threads.
\item \emph{private} --- per-thread memory with no inter-thread synchronization requirements.
\end{itemize}

\newpage
\section{Example pipeline}

an illustrative micro-architecture is proposed based on the classic 5-stage
RISC micro-architecture~\cite{patterson-hennessy} with the addition of an
\emph{operand fetch} stage and a \emph{constant memory} port. this revised
6-stage micro-architecture is composed of the following pipeline stages:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \textsf{IF} --- \emph{instruction fetch}: reads instructions from memory
into a fetch buffer.
\item \textsf{ID} --- \emph{instruction decode}: decodes instruction length, opcode,
and operands.
\item \textsf{OF} --- \emph{operand fetch}: reads operands from register file
and constant memory.
\item \textsf{EX} --- \emph{execute}: performs logical operations or
arithmetic on the operands.
\item \textsf{MA} --- \emph{memory access}: loads data from or stores data to memory.
\item \textsf{WB} --- \emph{writeback}: writes results back to the register file.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
a simplified micro-architecture using those pipeline stages might look like this:
this example omits hazard detection and forwarding logic for the sake of simplicity.
\\
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  stage/.style={rectangle, draw=black, thick, align=center},
  sidebox/.style={rectangle, draw=black, thick, fill=gray!10},
  arrow/.style={-{Latex[length=3mm, width=2mm]}, thick},
  node distance=12mm
]

% Pipeline stages
\tikzstyle{stage} = [draw, align=left, text width=40mm, minimum height=5mm, inner sep=2mm]
\tikzstyle{sidebox} = [draw, fill=black!5, align=right, text width=40mm, minimum height=5mm, inner sep=2mm]

\node[stage] (IF) {\textsf{IF} -- instruction fetch};
\node[stage, below=of IF] (ID) {\textsf{ID} -- instruction decode};
\node[stage, below=of ID] (OF) {\textsf{OF} -- operand fetch};
\node[stage, below=of OF] (EX) {\textsf{EX} -- execute};
\node[stage, below=of EX] (MA) {\textsf{MA} -- memory access};
\node[stage, below=of MA] (WB) {\textsf{WB} -- writeback};

% Arrows between pipeline stages
\draw[arrow] (IF) -- (ID);
\draw[arrow] (ID) -- (OF);
\draw[arrow] (OF) -- (EX);
\draw[arrow] (EX) -- (MA);
\draw[arrow] (MA) -- (WB);

% Side components
\node[sidebox, left=2.8cm of IF] (instrmem) {instruction memory -- \textsf{IM}};
\draw[->] (instrmem.east) -- (IF.west);

\node[sidebox, left=2.8cm of OF] (constmem) {constant memory -- \textsf{KM}};
\draw[->] ([yshift=1mm]constmem.east) -- ([yshift=1mm]OF.west);

\node[sidebox, left=2.8cm of MA] (datamem) {data memory -- \textsf{DM}};
\draw[<->] (datamem.east) -- (MA.west);

\node[sidebox, left=2.8cm of WB] (regfile) {register file -- \textsf{RF}};
\draw[->] ([yshift=1mm]regfile.east) -- ++(1.5,0) -- ++(0,5.5) -- ([yshift=-1mm]OF.west);
\draw[<-] ([yshift=-1mm]regfile.east) -- ([yshift=-1mm]WB.west);

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{sample 6-stage micro-architecture with support for constant memory.}
\label{fig:glyph-6-stage-uarch}
\end{figure}

\chapter{System}

\section{User-level registers}
the architecture provides several user-level status and control registers
for floating-point status and control, clock time, clock frequency,
thread address and domain.
\\
\par\noindent
user-level status and control registers can be read and written using the
\texttt{sysread}, \texttt{syswrite}, \texttt{sysset}, and \texttt{sysclear}
instructions. user-level status and control registers are available in
contexts where their associated bit is enabled in \texttt{scontrol}.

\subsubsection{user-level registers}
the following table lists the user-level registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{floating-point unit registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x00} & fpstatus  & floating-point status \\
\texttt{0x01} & fpcontrol & floating-point control \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{clock and frequency registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x02} & ctime     & clock time \\
\texttt{0x03} & cfreq     & clock frequency \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{thread address registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x04} & addr      & user thread address \\
\texttt{0x05} & domain    & user thread domain \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{user-level registers}
\label{tab:user-level-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{floating-point unit registers}
the section describes the user-level floating-point unit registers.

\subsubsection{floating-point status (\texttt{fpstatus})}
\texttt{fpstatus} is a read-only register containing status
information for floating-point operations. the \texttt{Z, O, U, X,}
and \texttt{I} fields contain accrued floating-point exceptions.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.F} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (31.5,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{I}};
\draw [black!95] (30,0)    rectangle (31,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (30.5,1.25)  {\textsf{1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (30.5,0.25)  {\texttt{X}};
\draw [black!95] (29,0)    rectangle (30,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (29.5,1.25)  {\textsf{2}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (29.5,0.25)  {\texttt{U}};
\draw [black!95] (28,0)    rectangle (29,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (28.5,1.25)  {\textsf{3}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.5,0.25)  {\texttt{O}};
\draw [black!95] (27,0)    rectangle (28,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (27.5,1.25)  {\textsf{4}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (27.5,0.25)  {\texttt{Z}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & I   & invalid operation \\
\verb|1 << 1| & X   & inexact result \\
\verb|1 << 2| & U   & numeric underflow \\
\verb|1 << 3| & O   & numeric overflow \\
\verb|1 << 4| & Z   & divide by zero \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point status flags}
\label{tab:floating-point-status-flags}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{floating-point control (\texttt{fpcontrol})}
\texttt{fpcontrol} is a read-write register containing control
information for floating-point operations. the \texttt{RM} field
contains the current floating-point rounding mode. the \texttt{Z}
field enables \emph{flush-to-zero} and \emph{denormals-are-zero}.
the \texttt{N} field enables \emph{not-strict-ieee754}, allowing
\emph{fused-multiply-add} and other optimizations that may affect
precision. this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.F} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (31.5,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{Z}};
\draw [black!95] (30,0)    rectangle (31,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (30.5,1.25)  {\textsf{1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (30.5,0.25)  {\texttt{N}};
\draw [black!95] (28,0)    rectangle (30,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (29.5,1.25)  {\textsf{2}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (28.5,1.25)  {\textsf{3}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (29,0.25)  {\texttt{RM}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & Z   & \emph{flush-to-zero} and \emph{denormals-are-zero} \\
\verb|1 << 1| & N   & \emph{not-strict-ieee754} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point control flags}
\label{tab:floating-point-control-flags}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|0| & RN   & round to nearest (even) \\
\verb|1| & RD   & round down (towards $-\infty$) \\
\verb|2| & RU   & round up (towards $+\infty$) \\
\verb|3| & RZ   & round towards zero (truncate) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{floating-point control round mode field}
\label{tab:floating-point-control-round-mode-field}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{clock and frequency registers}
the section describes the user-level clock and frequency registers.

\subsubsection{clock time (\texttt{ctime})}
\texttt{ctime} is a read-only register containing the wall-clock
tick counter since power on in clock tick units denoted by \texttt{cfreq}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.T} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{clock time}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{clock frequency (\texttt{cfreq})}
\texttt{cfreq} is a read-only register containing the wall-clock
tick interval in picoseconds; \(\frac{10^{12}}{f} \) where \( f \)
is the frequency in Hertz (Hz).
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.T} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{clock frequency}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsection{thread address registers}
the section describes the user-level thread address registers.

\subsubsection{thread address (\texttt{addr})}
\texttt{addr} is a read-only register containing an alias of the unique
system-wide topological address for this thread from \texttt{saddr}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.D} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (0.5,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16.25,0.25)  {\emph{thread address}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (0.25,0.25)  {\texttt{0}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{thread domain (\texttt{domain})}
\texttt{domain} is a read-only register containing an alias of the
system-wide domain address for this thread from \texttt{sdomain}.
this register is visible if \texttt{scontrol.D} is enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (0.5,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16.25,0.25)  {\emph{thread domain}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (0.25,0.25)  {\texttt{1}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\section{System registers}
the architecture provides several privileged status and control
registers for trap handling, system configuration, address translation,
timers, interrupts, and debugging.
\\
\par\noindent
privileged system-level status and control registers can be read and written
using the \texttt{sysread}, \texttt{syswrite}, \texttt{sysset}, and
\texttt{sysclear} instructions. privileged system registers are accessible
from executable pages with system or physical address spaces.

\subsubsection{privileged registers}
the following table lists the privileged trap, system, debug and machine
registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{privileged trap registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x10} & tstatus   & trap status \\
\texttt{0x11} & tcontrol  & trap control \\
\texttt{0x12} & tscratch  & trap scratch \\
\texttt{0x13} & tcause    & trap cause \\
\texttt{0x14} & tvalue    & trap value \\
\texttt{0x15} & tack      & trap acknowledge \\
\texttt{0x16} & tepc      & trap exception program counter  \\
\texttt{0x17} & teib      & trap exception immediate base \\
\texttt{0x18} & thpc      & trap handler program counter  \\
\texttt{0x19} & thib      & trap handler immediate base \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{privileged system registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x21} & scontrol  & system control \\
\texttt{0x22} & sfeature  & system feature \\
\texttt{0x23} & sversion  & system version \\
\texttt{0x24} & sptr      & system page table root \\
\texttt{0x25} & saddr     & system thread address \\
\texttt{0x26} & sdomain   & system thread domain \\
\texttt{0x27} & starget   & system target address \\
\texttt{0x28} & smask     & system target mask \\
\texttt{0x29} & smessage  & system target message \\
\texttt{0x2a} & stimer    & system deadline timer \\
\texttt{0x2b} & sie       & system interrupt enable \\
\texttt{0x2c} & sip       & system interrupt pending \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{privileged debug registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x30} & dstatus    & debug status \\
\texttt{0x32} & dcycle     & debug cycle counter \\
\texttt{0x33} & dinst      & debug instruction counter \\
\texttt{0x34} & dstop      & debug stop instruction \\
\texttt{0x35} & dfetch     & debug monitor fetch address \\
\texttt{0x36} & dread      & debug monitor read address \\
\texttt{0x37} & dwrite     & debug monitor write address \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{privileged registers}
\label{tab:privileged-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{privileged trap registers}
the section describes the privileged trap registers.

\subsubsection{trap status (\texttt{tstatus})}
\texttt{tstatus} is a read-only register containing trap status.
the \texttt{T} field indicates whether a timer interrupt is pending.
the \texttt{V} field indicates whether a virtual interrupt is pending.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (31.5,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{T}};
\draw [black!95] (30,0)    rectangle (31,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (30.5,1.25)  {\textsf{1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (30.5,0.25)  {\texttt{V}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & T   & timer interrupt pending \\
\verb|1 << 1| & V   & virtual interrupt pending \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap status fields}
\label{tab:trap-status-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap control (\texttt{tcontrol})}
\texttt{tcontrol} is a read-write register containing control bits. the
\texttt{I} field is read-write and controls whether system interrupts
are enabled.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (31.5,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{I}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & I   & system interrupt enable \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap control fields}
\label{tab:trap-control-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap scratch (\texttt{tscratch})}
\texttt{tscratch} is a read-write save register for use during trap handling.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{XLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{scratch value}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsubsection{trap cause (\texttt{tcause})}
\texttt{tcause} is a read-only register that contains the cause of the
current trap.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (29,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (29,1.25)  {\textsf{5}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (30.5,0.25)  {\emph{cause}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{system exceptions}} \\ \hline
\verb|1| & break-instruction   & break instruction exception \\
\verb|2| & illegal-instruction & illegal instruction exception \\
\verb|3| & debug-monitor       & debug monitor exception \\
\verb|4| & misaligned-fetch    & fetch misaligned \\
\verb|5| & misaligned-load     & load misaligned \\
\verb|6| & misaligned-store    & store misaligned \\
\verb|7| & access-fault-fetch  & fetch access fault \\
\verb|8| & access-fault-load   & load access fault \\
\verb|9| & access-fault-store  & store access fault \\
\verb|10| & page-fault-fetch   & fetch page fault \\
\verb|11| & page-fault-load    & load page fault \\
\verb|12| & page-fault-store   & store page fault \\
\verb|13| & capability-fault   & capability fault \\
\verb|14| & domain-fault       & domain fault \\
\verb|15| & machine-fault      & machine fault \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{system interrupts}} \\ \hline
\verb|30| & timer-interrupt     & timer interrupt \\
\verb|31| & virtual-interrupt   & virtual interrupt \\
\hline
\verb|32...63| & interrupt-n    & interrupt pins 0 -- 31 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{trap causes}
\label{tab:trap-causes}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{trap value (\texttt{tvalue})}
\texttt{tvalue} is a read-only register containing the identity of the trap
and may contain:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item the faulting instruction word for \emph{break} and \emph{illegal instruction exceptions}.
\item the faulting address for \emph{misaligned}, \emph{access} and \emph{page faults}.
\item the masked thread or domain address for \emph{virtual interrupts}.
\item the system clock time for \emph{timer interrupts}.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{XLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{trap value}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsubsection{trap acknowledge (\texttt{tack})}
\texttt{tack} is a write-only register where the trap cause is written
to acknowledge the trap so that interrupts are not delivered
while state is being saved, and for double-faults to be detected.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (29,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (29,1.25)  {\textsf{5}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (30.5,0.25)  {\emph{cause}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{trap exception program counter (\texttt{tepc})}
\texttt{tepc} is a read-only register which contains the \emph{program
counter} address before the trap.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{program counter}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{trap exception immediate base (\texttt{teib})}
\texttt{teib} is a read-only register which contains the \emph{immediate
base} address before the trap.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{immediate base}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{trap handler program counter (\texttt{thpc})}
\texttt{thpc} is a read-write register containing the address of the
system trap handler routine.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{program counter}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{trap handler immediate base (\texttt{thib})}
\texttt{thib} is a read-write register containing the address of the
system trap handler constants.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{immediate base}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system page table root (\texttt{sptr})}
\texttt{sptr} is a read-write register containing the page table root
physical address for address translation on this thread. the \emph{ASID}
field contains the address space identifier for this thread.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (26,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (26,1.25)  {\textsf{11}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (29,0.25)  {\emph{ASID}};
\draw [black!95] (8.0,0)   rectangle (26,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (26,1.25)  {\textsf{12}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (8,1.25)  {\textsf{47}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (17.0,0.25)  {\emph{physical page number}};
\draw [black!95] (0.0,0)   rectangle (8.0,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (8,1.25)  {\textsf{48}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{63}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (4.0,0.25)  {\emph{sign extended}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system thread address (\texttt{saddr})}
\texttt{saddr} is a read-only register containing the unique system-wide
topological address for this thread. the system-wide topological
address is used as a target for inter-thread virtual interrupts. this
address is uniquely assigned during system initialization.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (0.5,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16.25,0.25)  {\emph{thread address}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (0.25,0.25)  {\texttt{0}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system thread domain (\texttt{sdomain})}
\texttt{sdomain} is a read-only register containing the system-wide
domain address for this thread. the system-wide domain address
is used as a target for inter-thread virtual interrupts. this address
can alias. this address is assigned during context switching.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (0.5,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16.25,0.25)  {\emph{thread domain}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (0.25,0.25)  {\texttt{1}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsubsection{system target address (\texttt{starget})}
\texttt{starget} is a read-write register containing the system-wide
target address of a set of threads to send virtual interrupts to.
this register must be set before writing to \texttt{smessage}.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item all-zeros is the \emph{boot processor}.
\item all-ones is the \emph{broadcast address}.
\item zero in the \emph{A} field is a \emph{topological address}.
\item one in the \emph{A} field is a \emph{domain address}.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (0.5,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16.25,0.25)  {\emph{target address}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (0.25,0.25)  {\texttt{A}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system target mask (\texttt{smask})}
\texttt{smask} is a read-write register containing the system-wide
address mask of a set of threads to send virtual interrupts to.
this register must be set before writing to \texttt{smessage}.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{target mask}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system target message (\texttt{smessage})}
\texttt{smessage} is a write-only register that causes an edge-triggered
virtual interrupt to be queued to the thread set in \texttt{starget}.
virtual interrupts are marked pending in the \texttt{V} field of the
\texttt{tstatus} register for the target threads.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{MLEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{message signal}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system deadline timer (\texttt{stimer})}
\texttt{stimer} is a read-write register containing the deadline timer
for this thread. when the system clock reaches the value in the
register, a \emph{timer interrupt} is triggered.
timer interrupts are marked pending in the \texttt{T} field of the
\texttt{tstatus} register.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (0,1.25)  {\textsf{ALEN-1}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (16,0.25)  {\emph{clock time}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsubsection{system interrupt enable (\texttt{sie})}
\texttt{sie} is a read-write register that contains interrupt enable flags
for 32 wired interrupt pins.
\\
\par\noindent

\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{system interrupt pending (\texttt{sip})}
\texttt{sip} is a read-only register that contains interrupt pending flags
for 32 wired interrupt pins.
\\

\subsubsection{debug monitor fetch address (\texttt{dfetch})}
\texttt{dfetch} is a read-write register containing a memory fetch address
to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug monitor

\\

address to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug
monitor exception} for a matching memory load address.
\\

\texttt{dwrite} is a read-write register containing the memory store
address to monitor for and halt execution on and raise a \emph{debug
monitor exception} for a matching memory store address.
\\

\newpage
\section{Capability extension}

the architecture provides an optional capabilities extension that
provides a capability check for permissions and capabilities enabled by
\emph{page table colors} in page table entries. color permissions mask
page table permissions based on \emph{page table color}. color
capabilities restrict access to system, domain, capability and machine
registers based on \emph{page table color}.
\\
\par\noindent
the following tables list the privileged capability system parameters:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{95mm}{|l|X|}
\hline
\textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
$\mathit{ncol}$      & the number of architectural colors \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{capability parameters}
\label{tab:capability-parameters}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{capability registers}
the following tables list the privileged capability system registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} & \textbf{depends} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{capability control registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x41} & ccontrol       & capability control & \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{capability permission registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x43} & colorread      & color read permissions & \\
\texttt{0x44} & colorwrite     & color write permissions & \\
\texttt{0x45} & colorexec      & color exec permissions & \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{capability role registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x46} & colorsys       & color system role & \\
\texttt{0x47} & colordom       & color domain role & domain \\
\texttt{0x48} & colorcap       & color capability role & \\
\texttt{0x49} & colormac       & color machine role & machine \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{capability matrix registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x100...0x10f} & colormatread$[n]$   & color matrix read permissions & \\
\texttt{0x200...0x20f} & colormatwrite$[n]$   & color matrix write permissions & \\
\texttt{0x300...0x30f} & colormatexec$[n]$   & color matrix exec permissions & \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{capability registers}
\label{tab:capability-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{capability control registers}

this section describes the \emph{capability control registers} which enable
the capability extension.

\subsubsection{capability control (\texttt{ccontrol})}
\texttt{ccontrol} is a read-write register containing capability control bits.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base] at (31.5,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{C}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & C   & capability check enable \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{color control fields}
\label{tab:color-control-fields}
\end{table}

\subsection{capability permission registers}

this section describes the \emph{capability permission registers} which
define \emph{page table color} permission masks for page table entries.
when \texttt{ccontrol.C} is set, the \emph{capability permission registers}
are combined with page table permissions to limit maximum \emph{read},
\emph{write}, or \emph{execute} permission based on page table color.

\subsubsection{color read permission (\texttt{colorread})}
\texttt{colorread} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing read permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check loads from pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{read perms}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color write permission (\texttt{colorwrite})}
\texttt{colorwrite} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing write permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check stores to pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{write perms}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color exec permission (\texttt{colorexec})}
\texttt{colorexec} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing execute permissions for \emph{color-x},
used to check execution in pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{exec perms}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsection{capability role registers}

this section describes the \emph{capability role registers}.
the \emph{capability role registers} describe access to privileged
system registers and instructions based on \emph{page table color}.
when \texttt{ccontrol.C} is set, privileged system registers and
instructions and will cause capability traps for illegal accesses.
system registers and instructions are restricted based on the
following roles: \emph{system}, \emph{domain}, \emph{capability},
and \emph{machine}.

\subsubsection{color system role (\texttt{colorsys})}
\texttt{colorsys} is a read-write register containing an array of
\textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing system role for
\emph{color-x}, used to check access to privileged trap
registers, privileged system registers and privileged debug registers
from executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{system role}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color domain role (\texttt{colordom})}
\texttt{colordom} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing domain role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged domain registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{domain role}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color capability role (\texttt{colorcap})}
\texttt{colorcap} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing capability role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged capability registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{capability role}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color machine role (\texttt{colormac})}
\texttt{colormac} is a read-write register containing an array
of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits containing machine role for \emph{color-x}
that are used to check access to privileged machine registers from
executable pages with a matching page table color.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{machine role}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\newpage
\subsection{capability matrix registers}

this section describes the color matrix registers. the \emph{capability
matrix registers} are permission registers that protect access to
memory based on a combination of \emph{source} and \emph{destination}
page table color and \emph{read}, \emph{write}, or \emph{execute}
permissions. when \texttt{ccontrol.C} is set, memory accesses and
branches to memory addresses in pages with matching \emph{source} and
\emph{destination} page table colors, and wider permissions, will cause
capability traps.

\subsubsection{color matrix read permissions (\texttt{colormatread}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatread}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with read
permissions, used to check loads from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to memory addresses in
memory pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{read perms}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color matrix write permissions (\texttt{colormatwrite}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatwrite}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with write
permissions, used to check stores from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to memory addresses in
memory pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base west] at (24,1.25)  {$\mathit{ncol}$};
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (28.0,0.25)  {\emph{write perms}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{color matrix exec permissions (\texttt{colormatexec}$[\mathit{ncol}]$)}
\texttt{colormatexec}$[\mathit{ncol}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ncol}} read-write
registers containing an array of \textbf{\emph{ncol}} bits with exec
permissions, used to check branches from executable pages with a
matching register \emph{page table color} to branch addresses in
executable pages with a matching \emph{page table color} index.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (24,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\node [font=\tiny,anchor=base east] at (32,1.25)  {\textsf{0}};

\newpage
\section{Domain extension}

the architecture provides an optional domain~\cite{barham2003xen}
extension for virtual machines. the extension adds registers for virtual
domain addresses, trap routing, page translation, and context switching
for system traps, domain traps, capability traps and machine traps.
\\
\par\noindent
the following tables list the privileged domain system parameters:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{95mm}{|l|X|}
\hline
\textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
$\mathit{ndom}$      & the number of architectural domains \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{domain parameters}
\label{tab:domain-parameters}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{domain registers}
the following tables list the privileged domain system registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} & \textbf{depends} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{domain control registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x51} & domcontrol & domain control & \\
\texttt{0x53} & domlast  & domain number last & \\
\texttt{0x54} & domcurr  & domain number current & \\
\texttt{0x55} & domnext  & domain number next & \\
\texttt{0x500...0x50f} & dommatrix$[n]$ & domain matrix & \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{domain routing registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x600...0x60f} & domsys$[n]$    & domain system default domain & \\
\texttt{0x700...0x70f} & domdom$[n]$    & domain domain default domain & \\
\texttt{0x800...0x80f} & domcap$[n]$    & domain capability default domain & capability \\
\texttt{0x900...0x90f} & dommac$[n]$    & domain machine default domain & machine \\
\texttt{0xa00...0xa0f} & dommsg$[n]$    & domain message default domain & \\
\hline
\multicolumn{4}{|l|}{\textbf{domain context registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0xb00...0xb0f} & domscratch$[n]$& domain scratch & \\
\texttt{0xc00...0xc0f} & domaddr$[n]$   & domain address & \\
\texttt{0xd00...0xd0f} & domptr$[n]$    & domain page table root & \\
\texttt{0xe00...0xe0f} & domthpc$[n]$   & domain trap handler program counter & \\
\texttt{0xf00...0xf0f} & domthib$[n]$   & domain trap handler immediate base & \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{domain registers}
\label{tab:domain-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{domain control registers}

this section describes the \emph{domain control registers}, which enable
the domain extension, provide access to the last and current domain number,
control the next domain number, and control valid domain transitions.

\subsubsection{domain control (\texttt{domcontrol})}
\texttt{domcontrol} is a read-write register containing domain control bits.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);
\draw [black!95] (31,0)    rectangle (32,1);

\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (31.5,0.25)  {\texttt{V}};
\end{tikzpicture}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\verb|1 << 0| & V   & virtual domains enable \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{domain control fields}
\label{tab:domain-control-fields}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{domain number last (\texttt{domlast})}
\texttt{domlast} is a read-only register containing the domain number
of the last domain.
\\

of the current domain.

\texttt{dommatrix}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write

\subsection{domain context registers}

this section describes the \emph{domain context registers} that control
virtual interrupt routing, page translation and trap entry functions for
domain context switching.

\subsubsection{domain scratch (\texttt{domscratch}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domscratch}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the scratch values associated with each
domain.
\\

\end{tikzpicture}

\subsubsection{domain address (\texttt{domaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
\texttt{domaddr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$ are \textbf{\emph{ndom}} read-write
registers containing an array of the domain addresses associated with each
domain.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]
\draw [black!95] (0,0)    rectangle (32,1);

\subsubsection{domain page table root (\texttt{domptr}$[\mathit{ndom}]$)}
