\section{Machine extension}

the architecture provides an optional machine extension that includes
machine-level registers for physical memory protection and
software-defined memory management.

\subsubsection{machine registers}
the following tables list the privileged machine registers:

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{125mm}{|l|l|X|}
\hline
\textbf{no.} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{machine control registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x61} & mcontrol  & machine control \\
\texttt{0x62} & mfeature  & machine feature \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{machine memory registers}} \\
\hline
\texttt{0x63} & mtlbkey   & machine translation lookaside buffer key \\
\texttt{0x64} & mtlbfmt   & machine translation lookaside buffer format \\
\texttt{0x65} & mtlbent   & machine translation lookaside buffer entry \\
\hline
\end{tabularx}
\caption{machine registers}
\label{tab:machine-registers}
\end{table}

\newpage

the section describes the privileged machine registers for machine
control and features.

\subsection{machine memory registers}
the section describes the privileged machine registers for address
translation. the registers in this section enable a \emph{machine-fault}
trap handler to perform address translation by walking the page table
structures and populating the TLB\footnote{Translation Lookaside Buffer}
as part of a software-defined MMU\footnote{Memory Management Unit}.

\subsubsection{machine translation lookaside buffer key (\texttt{mtlbkey})}
\texttt{mtlbkey} is a read-only register containing the machine fault
translation virtual address for the current page translation miss fault.
\\
\par\noindent
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.45]

\subsubsection{machine translation lookaside buffer format (\texttt{mtlbfmt})}
\texttt{mtlbfmt} is a read-write register containing the machine fault
address space identifier and page table format for the current page
translation miss fault.
\\

\label{tab:64-bit-machine-translation-lookaside-buffer-format-field}

\subsubsection{machine translation lookaside buffer entry (\texttt{mtlbent})}
\texttt{mtlbent} is a read-write register containing the machine fault
translation lookaside buffer entry for the current page translation miss
fault. this register is populated by the page translation miss fault
handler with a physical page number, a page color, plus several permission
and metadata bits in the same format as the metadata bits defined for page
table entries. table~\ref{tab:64-bit-page-table-entry-fields} in the address
translation section describes the metadata bits.

\section{Environment extension}

the architecture provides an optional environment extension that includes
machine-level registers for diagnostics, warm reset, cold reset, and power off.

\subsubsection{environment registers}
the following tables list the privileged environment registers:

\newpage
\subsection{environment control registers}
the section describes the privileged registers for environment control.

\subsubsection{environment control (\texttt{econtrol})}
\texttt{econtrol} is a read-write register used to issue machine-level
environment control commands. the \texttt{OP} field contains an opcode
with values for: \emph{diagnostic}, \emph{warm reset}, \emph{cold reset}
and \emph{power off}. the \emph{cause code} field is recorded in an
environment log.
\\

\section{Address translation}

the architecture provides for page-based virtual to physical address
translation using a page table \emph{trie structure} composed of index
pages containing arrays of page table entries. a page walker reads the
structure from the root pointer to leaf entries to translate virtual
addresses into physical addresses. the architecture introduces the
concept of a \emph{translation address} which is an address boxed with
an \emph{address space prefix} (\texttt{AS}) designed to provide a
canonical address form for user and system virtual addresses as
well as physical addresses.

\subsection{page table structure}
the section describes the dimensions for memory address translation
and the \emph{trie-based} page table structure pointed to by the
\emph{system page table root} (\texttt{sptr}) register.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Page} & \textbf{Page} & \textbf{Page} & \textbf{Virtual} &
\textbf{Page} & \\
\textbf{Table} & \textbf{Number} & \textbf{Offset} & \textbf{Address} &
\textbf{Index} & \textbf{Page} \\
\textbf{Levels} & \textbf{Bits} & \textbf{Bits} & \textbf{Bits} &
\textbf{Entries} & \textbf{Sizes} \\
\hline
4 & 9 & 12 & 44 --- 48 & 512 & 4KiB, 2Mib, 1GiB \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{64-bit page table dimensions}
\label{tab:64-bit-page-table-dimensions}
\end{table}

\subsection{page table entries}
page table entries are grouped into arrays within index pages which are
selected by an index derived from a portion of the virtual address. each
entry contains a physical page number, a page color, plus several permission
and metadata bits. the physical page number points to the next page
table level for \emph{pointer} entries or the translated physical address
for \emph{leaf} entries.

\subsection{page table addresses}
\label{sec:page-table-addresses}
the page table translation system has three types of addresses:
translation addresses, virtual addresses and physical addresses.
page table translation addresses have a 1 --- 5 bit address space
prefix to denote user, system, physical, group shared and thread
private address spaces. the page table translation and lookup virtual
address structure is as follows:

\subsection{page table translation}
the page table walker performs page table lookup to translate virtual
addresses into physical addresses. the page table walker reads the page
table root pointer then walks the \emph{trie-based} page table structure
to find a leaf entry containing a physical page address.
\\
\par\noindent
page table entries with the \emph{read, write} and \emph{exec} permission
bits clear are interpreted as \emph{pointer} entries and contain a pointer
to the next-level index page, otherwise they are interpreted as \emph{leaf}
entries with a final translation. entries with the \emph{write} bit set
must have the \emph{read} bit set. entries without the \emph{accessed} bit
set will fault on read accesses. entries without the \emph{dirty} bit set
will fault on write accesses. the \emph{global} bit prevents \emph{sptr.ASID}
from being associated with TLB entries. the \emph{color} field is reserved
for use by system software and the capabilities extension.
\\
\par\noindent
if the physical permissions feature \emph{mfeature.P} is present and
\emph{mcontrol.P} is enabled, then physical pages must be present in the
page table with a self-mapping of their physical address prefixed with
$(\mathit{AS} = \mathit{physical})$ leading to a leaf page table entry
containing its own address. this is to allow physical page permissions
to be checked. in addition to this, the physical self-mappings for physical
pages containing page table pages must have the \emph{translate} bit set.
\\
\par\noindent
the page table lookup and virtual to physical address translation process
are as follows:

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{find page table entry for translation address}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{find\_page}{$\mathit{type},\mathit{level},\mathit{ppn},\mathit{ta}$}
  \State $\mathit{shift} \gets \mathit{level} \times
    \mathit{pn\_bits} + \mathit{po\_bits}$
  \Comment{calculate page number shift}
  \State $\mathit{tpn} \gets (\mathit{ta} >> \mathit{shift}) \land
    ((1 << \mathit{pn\_bits}) - 1)$
  \Comment{calculate translation page number}
  \State $\mathit{pte} \gets \Call{load}{(\mathit{ppn}
    << \mathit{po\_bits}) + \mathit{tpn} \times \mathit{sizeof(PTE)}}$
  \Comment{load page table entry}
  \State $\mathit{leaf} \gets
    \mathit{pte.R} \lor \mathit{pte.W} \lor \mathit{pte.X}$
  \Comment{page table entry node type}
  \State $\mathit{sm} \gets
    \mathit{R} \lor \mathit{W} \lor \mathit{X}$
  \Comment{default self map page table entry}
  \If {$\mathit{mfeature.P} \land \mathit{mcontrol.P} \land \mathit{type} =
      \mathit{virtual}$}
    \State $\mathit{pa} \gets \mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{po\_shift}$
    \State $(\mathit{sm},\mathit{level}) \gets \Call{find\_page}{%
      \mathit{physical},\mathit{num\_levels}-1,\mathit{sptr.ppn},\mathit{pa}}$
    \Comment{load self mapping}
    \If {$(\mathit{leaf} \land \mathit{pte.ppn} \ne \mathit{sm.ppn}) \lor
      (\lnot\mathit{leaf} \land \lnot\mathit{sm.T})$}
      \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
      \Comment{invalid self mapping}
    \EndIf
  \EndIf
  \If {$\lnot\mathit{leaf} \land \mathit{level} > 0$}
    \State \Return $\Call{find\_page}{\mathit{type},\mathit{level}-1,%
      \mathit{pte.ppn},\mathit{ta}}$
    \Comment{follow pointer entry}
  \ElsIf {$\lnot\mathit{leaf}$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{leaf entry not found}
  \EndIf
  \State \Return $(\mathit{pte}, \mathit{sm}, \mathit{level})$
  \Comment{return page table entry}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{translate virtual address to physical address}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{translate}{$\mathit{op}, \mathit{va}$}
  \If {$\lnot\Call{check\_canonical}{\mathit{va}, \mathit{va\_bits}}$}
    \Comment{check address is sign-extended}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
  \EndIf
  \State $(\mathit{pte}, \mathit{sm}, \mathit{level}) \gets \Call{find\_page}{
    \mathit{virtual},\mathit{num\_levels}-1,\mathit{sptr.ppn},\mathit{va}}$
  \Comment{find page table entry}
  \State $\mathit{mask} \gets
    ((1 << (\mathit{level} \times \mathit{pn\_bits} + \mathit{po\_bits})) - 1)$
  \State $\mathit{caps} \gets
    (\mathit{R} \land \mathit{colorread[pte.color]}) \lor
    (\mathit{W} \land \mathit{colorwrite[pte.color]}) \lor
    (\mathit{X} \land \mathit{colorexec[pte.color]})$
  \State $\mathit{user} \gets
    \lnot((\mathit{va} >> (\mathit{va\_bits} - 1)) \land 1)$
  \If {$(\lnot\mathit{pte.V}) \lor (\mathit{pte.W} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.R})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid write must have read}
  \ElsIf{$(\mathit{op.R} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.R}) \lor (\mathit{op.W} \land
    \lnot\mathit{pte.W}) \lor (\mathit{op.X} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.X})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid permissions}
  \ElsIf{$(\mathit{sm.R} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.R}) \lor (\mathit{sm.W} \land
    \lnot\mathit{pte.W}) \lor (\mathit{sm.X} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.X})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid self map}
  \ElsIf{$(\mathit{cap.R} \land \mathit{pte.R}) \lor (\mathit{cap.W} \land
    \mathit{pte.W}) \lor (\mathit{cap.X} \land \mathit{pte.X})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid capabilities}
  \ElsIf {$(\mathit{op.R} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.A}) \lor
      (\mathit{op.W} \land \lnot\mathit{pte.D})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid accessed or dirty}
  \ElsIf {$\lnot\mathit{tstatus.U} \land \lnot\mathit{scontrol.E} \land
      \mathit{user} \land \mathit{op.X}$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid user page execute}
  \ElsIf {$\lnot\mathit{tstatus.U} \land \lnot\mathit{scontrol.A} \land
      \mathit{user} \land \mathit{op.(R \lor W})$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid user page access}
  \ElsIf {($\mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{po\_bits}) \land \mathit{mask} \ne 0$}
    \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{fault}
    \Comment{invalid superpage alignment}
  \EndIf
  \State \Return $(\mathit{pte.ppn} << \mathit{po\_bits}) +
    (\mathit{va} \land \mathit{mask})$
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\section{Interrupt handling}

the glyph interrupt system is designed to support synchronous and
asynchronous traps to divert control flow for instruction execution
exceptions and synchronous or asynchronous message delivery interrupts
for inter-domain messages, timers, and peripheral interrupt pins.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item synchronous traps due to instruction execution.
\item synchronous traps due to locally scoped inter-domain messages.
\item asynchronous traps due to interrupts from timers or interrupt pins.
\item asynchronous traps due to globally scoped inter-domain messages.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{trap handlers}

a trap handler is the control flow entry point to a function that handles
synchronous traps caused by instruction execution faults and asynchronous
traps caused by external interrupts. when a trap is received the processor
saves the \emph{program counter} and \emph{immediate base} registers into
\{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \}, then branches to the addresses in
\{ \texttt{thpc}, \texttt{thib} \}.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \} contains the address of the trapping instruction.
\item \{ \texttt{thpc}, \texttt{thib} \} contains the address of the trap handler.
\item \texttt{tstatus} indicates if an asynchronous interrupt is pending.
\item \texttt{tcontrol} is used to enable or disable asynchronous interrupts.
\item \texttt{tscratch} is a scratch register reserved for trap handlers.
\item \texttt{tcause} contains the type code of the current trap.
\item \texttt{tvalue} contains identity information for the current trap.
\item \texttt{tack} is used to acknowledge asynchronous traps.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{synchronous traps}

synchronous traps arise from instruction execution due to internal events
such as: execution permissions, illegal instructions, debug monitor faults,
memory access faults, memory alignment faults, and page faults.
when synchronous traps occur, \{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \} contain
the address of the instruction that caused the trap.

\subsubsection{asynchronous traps}

asynchronous traps are generated by external events such as: interrupt
pins, message-signaled interrupts from other threads or domains on the
system, or deadline timer interrupts. when asynchronous traps occur,
\{ \texttt{tepc}, \texttt{teib} \} contain the address of the
instruction that was interrupted and must be returned to after
processing of the asynchronous trap.

\subsection{trap routing}

capability and domain registers are used to configure the trap routing
domains for context switches arising from interrupts and exceptions.
trap routing is configured by the hypervisor in the \texttt{domsys},
\texttt{domdom}, \texttt{domcap}, \texttt{dommac}, and \texttt{dommsg}
vectors, which contain the domain ids of domains that receive traps
for the \emph{system}, \emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine},
and \emph{message} roles respectively.
synchronous and asynchronous interrupts and exceptions are delivered
to the \emph{system}, \emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine},
and \emph{message} roles depending on the initiating event:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \emph{system role} receives system-level access exceptions.
\item \emph{domain role} receives domain-level access exceptions.
\item \emph{capability role} receives capability-level access exceptions.
\item \emph{machine role} receives machine-level access exceptions.
\item \emph{message role} receives asynchronous message exceptions.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{synchronous exceptions}

synchronous exceptions are grouped into \emph{system}, \emph{domain},
\emph{capability}, and \emph{machine} roles and are routed based on the
\texttt{domsys}, \texttt{domdom}, \texttt{domcap}, and \texttt{dommac}
vectors:
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{system exceptions:}
arising from access to system registers, illegal instruction
exceptions, break instruction exceptions, debug monitor exceptions,
address alignment faults, memory access faults, page faults arising
from page table entry configuration, and locally-scoped
\emph{system role} messages, also known as system calls.
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{capability exceptions:}
arising from access to capability registers, access checks due
to the configuration of the capability permission registers, capability
role registers, and the capability matrix, and locally-scoped
\emph{capability role} messages, also known as capability calls.
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{domain exceptions:}
arising from access to domain registers, domain matrix checks, and
locally-scoped \emph{domain role} messages, also known as hyper calls.
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{machine exceptions:}
faults arising from access to machine registers, translation lookaside
buffer refill faults, and locally-scoped \emph{machine role} messages,
also known as machine calls.

\subsection{asynchronous exceptions}

asynchronous exceptions use the \emph{message} role and are
routed based on the \texttt{dommsg} vector:
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{message exceptions:}
arising from interrupt pins, the deadline timer, and
globally-scoped inter-domain messages, also known as virtual interrupts
or message-signaled interrupts.

\newpage
\subsection{inter-domain messages}

inter-domain messages are used to send messages to threads locally
or globally. inter-domain messages are grouped into two categories.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item locally-scoped synchronous inter-domain messages.
\item globally-scoped asynchronous inter-domain messages.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
\textbf{locally-scoped inter-domain messages:} use the \emph{domain trap
routing tables} to route synchronous exceptions to the default domain
for a specific role on the local processor.
\\
\par\noindent
\textbf{globally-scoped inter-domain messages:} use the \texttt{starget},
\texttt{smask}, and \texttt{smessage} system registers, to multicast
asynchronous message-signaled interrupts over the processor cluster network
to the machine role of processors matching the advertised target thread or
domain address after applying the address mask.
\\
\par\noindent
the following table shows the inter-domain message instructions.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{capability} & \textbf{synchronous call} & \textbf{synchronous return} \\
\hline
\emph{system}      & \texttt{syscall} & \texttt{sysret} \\
\emph{domain}      & \texttt{domcall} & \texttt{domret} \\
\emph{capability}  & \texttt{capcall} & \texttt{capret} \\
\emph{machine}     & \texttt{maccall} & \texttt{macret} \\
\emph{message}     & -- & -- \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{inter-domain message instructions}
\label{tab:inter-domain-message-instructions}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the following table shows the inter-domain trap routing table keys.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{capability} & \textbf{synchronous call} & \textbf{synchronous return} \\
\hline
\emph{system}      & \texttt{domsys[domcurr]} & \texttt{domsys[domnext]} \\
\emph{domain}      & \texttt{domdom[domcurr]} & \texttt{domdom[domnext]} \\
\emph{capability}  & \texttt{domcap[domcurr]} & \texttt{domcap[domnext]} \\
\emph{machine}     & \texttt{dommac[domcurr]} & \texttt{dommac[domnext]} \\
\emph{message}     & \texttt{dommsg[domcurr]} & \texttt{dommsg[domnext]} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{inter-domain trap routing table keys}
\label{tab:inter-domain-trap-routing-table-keys}
\end{table}

\newpage
\subsection{wired interrupt pins}

there are 32 wired interrupt pins per processor. an interrupt is
generated upon an edge condition on the lowest-numbered interrupt pin
that is asserted. on signaled processors, \texttt{dommsg[domcurr]}
contains the message default domain used to route an asynchronous trap.
the processor initiates a \emph{domain context switch} to the
\emph{message default domain}, then branches to its trap handler.
\texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{interrupt-n} where \emph{n} is the pin
number and \texttt{tvalue} is set to zero.

\subsection{deadline timer interrupts}

deadline timer interrupts can be configured by setting the \texttt{stimer}
register. an interrupt is generated when $\mathtt{stimer} \ge \mathtt{ctime}$.
on the signaled processor, \texttt{dommsg[domcurr]}
contains the message default domain used to route an asynchronous trap.
the processor initiates a \emph{domain context switch} to the
\emph{message default domain}, then branches to its trap handler.
\texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{timer-interrupt} and \texttt{tvalue} is set
to the value in the \texttt{ctime} register.

\subsection{message signaled interrupts}

message-signaled interrupts, also known as globally-scoped inter-domain
messages, are multicast over the processor cluster network to initiate
asynchronous exceptions in the message role of selected threads
advertising the target thread or domain address after applying the
address mask.
\\
\par\noindent
the \emph{system} role of the initiating processor first disables
interrupts, then sets the \texttt{starget} and \texttt{smask} registers
with a target thread or domain address and mask, followed by writing
to the \texttt{smessage} register. a virtual interrupt is then multicast
to the set of threads matching the masked thread or domain address.
\\
\par\noindent
the following special addresses can be used.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{address} & \textbf{type} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{0\ldots0}& all-zeros & \emph{boot processor} \\
\texttt{1\ldots1} & all-ones & \emph{broadcast address} \\
\texttt{0?\ldots} & MSB-zero & \emph{topological address} \\
\texttt{1?\ldots} & MSB-one & \emph{domain address} \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{special inter-domain message addresses}
\label{tab:inter-domain-message-addresses}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
on signaled processors, \texttt{dommsg[domcurr]} contains the message
default domain used to route an asynchronous trap.
the processor initiates a \emph{domain context switch} to the
\emph{message default domain}, then branches to its trap handler.
\texttt{tcause} is set to \emph{virtual-interrupt} and \texttt{tvalue} is
set to the masked thread or domain address from the initiating processor.

\newpage
\subsection{domain context switch}

interrupt delivery due to synchronous and asynchronous exceptions
causes a domain switch to the trap routing domain for the role the
exception is routed to for the current domain: the \emph{system},
\emph{domain}, \emph{capability}, \emph{machine}, and \emph{message}
roles.

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \texttt{domsys[domcurr]} contains the system default domain.
\item \texttt{domdom[domcurr]} contains the domain default domain.
\item \texttt{domcap[domcurr]} contains the capability default domain.
\item \texttt{dommac[domcurr]} contains the machine default domain.
\item \texttt{dommsg[domcurr]} contains the message default domain.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following domain vectors hold state for a domain context switch.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{domain register} & \textbf{system register} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{domscratch[n]} & \texttt{tscratch} & trap scratch value \\
\texttt{domthpc[n]} & \texttt{thpc} & trap handler program counter \\
\texttt{domthib[n]} & \texttt{thib} & trap handler immediate base \\
\texttt{domaddr[n]} & \texttt{sdomain} & system domain address \\
\texttt{domptr[n]} & \texttt{sptr} & system page table root pointer \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{domain context switch registers}
\label{tab:domain-context-switch-registers}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the following procedure is used to perform a domain context switch.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\footnotesize
\caption{context switch to a domain and execute its trap handler}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Function{switch\_domain}{$\mathit{domain,cause,value}$}
    \If {$\lnot\mathit{dommatrix[domcurr][domain]}$}
    \Comment{check domain matrix}
        \State \textbf{raise} \textbf{domain-fault}
        \Comment{raise domain fault}
    \EndIf
    \State $\mathit{domlast} \gets \mathit{domcurr}$
    \Comment{save current domain to last}
    \State $\mathit{domnext} \gets \mathit{domcurr}$
    \Comment{save current domain to next}
    \State $\mathit{domcurr} \gets \mathit{domain}$
    \Comment{set new current domain}
    \State $\mathit{tepc} \gets \mathit{pc}$
    \Comment{save program counter register}
    \State $\mathit{teib} \gets \mathit{ib}$
    \Comment{save immediate base register}
    \State $\mathit{tcause} \gets \mathit{cause}$
    \Comment{set trap cause}
    \State $\mathit{tvalue} \gets \mathit{value}$
    \Comment{set trap value}
    \State $\mathit{tscratch}\gets \mathit{domscratch[domain]}$
    \Comment{restore trap scratch value}
    \State $\mathit{thpc} \gets \mathit{domthpc[domain]}$
    \Comment{restore trap handler program counter}
    \State $\mathit{thib} \gets \mathit{domthib[domain]}$
    \Comment{restore trap handler immediate base}
    \State $\mathit{sdomain} \gets \mathit{domaddr[domain]}$
    \Comment{restore system domain address}
    \State $\mathit{sptr} \gets \mathit{domptr[domain]}$
    \Comment{restore system page table root pointer}
    \State $\mathit{pc} \gets \mathit{thpc}$
    \Comment{set program counter to trap handler}
    \State $\mathit{ib} \gets \mathit{thib}$
    \Comment{set immediate base to trap handler}
\EndFunction
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\newpage
\section{Virtual machine}

glyph defines a base virtual machine model with a well-defined minimum set of
firmware interfaces, hardware description models and virtual devices for I/O.

\subsection{hypervisor}

the virtual machine defines several roles. the \emph{domain},
\emph{capability}, \emph{machine}, and \emph{message} roles are used by
the hypervisor to control domain switching, trap routing, execution
permissions and software-defined page-based virtual address translation.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{symbol} & \textbf{role} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{usr} & user       & execution of user-level programs \\
\texttt{sys} & system     & operating system software \\
\texttt{dom} & domain     & domain scheduling and routing \\
\texttt{cap} & capability & capabilities and permissions \\
\texttt{mac} & machine    & software-defined memory management \\
\texttt{msg} & message    & globally-scoped inter-domain messages \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine roles}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-roles}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the hypervisor is responsible for setting up the capability permission
registers, trap routing and context switching registers, and the initial
state of system registers to create threads in an isolated context before
handing over control to the firmware.

\subsection{firmware interface}

the base virtual machine model provides early boot interfaces to bootloaders
and operating systems using the Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI).
the following minimal set of UEFI protocols are required in the glyph base
virtual machine model.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{EFI\_LOADED\_IMAGE\_PROTOCOL}        & image context and load parameters \\
\texttt{EFI\_DEVICE\_PATH\_PROTOCOL}         & device and image location encoding \\
\texttt{EFI\_BLOCK\_IO\_PROTOCOL}            & block device access to boot media \\
\texttt{EFI\_SERIAL\_IO\_PROTOCOL}           & serial device access to console \\
\texttt{EFI\_SIMPLE\_FILE\_SYSTEM\_PROTOCOL} & filesystem access for boot media \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine UEFI protocols}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-uefi-protocols}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the firmware is responsible for setting up boot page tables with a
self-map of physical memory, enabling page-based virtual address
translation, providing the hardware description in device-tree format,
initializing devices required for loading the bootloader, then loading
and executing the bootloader by calling into \texttt{EfiMain}.
the firmware runs with the \emph{system} role.

\newpage
\subsection{hardware description}

the base virtual machine model uses device-tree to describe hardware
configuration properties such as physical memory layout, device identities
and properties such as memory-mapped I/O addresses, interrupt routing and
other configuration parameters.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{symbol} & \textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\textbf{DTS} & device tree source & the hardware description format \\
\textbf{DTB} & device tree binary & the compiled binary object \\
\textbf{FDT} & flattened device tree & the standard format used by bootloaders \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine hardware description}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-hardware-description}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the hardware description is responsible for providing a complete map of
physical memory and system devices and resources to the firmware and
system software. it encodes memory regions, bus hierarchies, interrupt
controllers, and device-specific configuration properties in device-tree
format. the firmware parses it at boot to initialize devices, and exposes
it to the bootloader and system software, ensuring a uniform view of
virtual hardware.

\subsection{virtual devices}

the base virtual machine model uses OASIS Virtual I/O (VirtIO) as an
interface for emulated devices inside glyph virtual machines. the
following minimal set of VirtIO device models are required in the
base virtual machine model.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{name} & \textbf{description} \\
\hline
\texttt{virtio-net}     & network device \\
\texttt{virtio-block}   & block device \\
\texttt{virtio-console} & console device \\
\texttt{virtio-rng}     & random number generator \\
\texttt{virtio-9p}      & 9p filesystem \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{glyph virtual machine virtual devices}
\label{tab:glyph-virtual-machine-virtual-devices}
\end{table}

\par\noindent
the virtual devices provide the interface between the guest operating
system and emulated I/O hardware. each device implements a VirtIO protocol
and exposes standard operations such as configuration, data transfer, and
interrupt signaling. the firmware and system software device drivers use
the virtual devices to perform I/O in a platform-independent way.

\chapter{Instructions} \label{sec:instructions}

\section{Instruction listing --- 16-bit}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{break}
\insnimmnine{uimm}{00000}{\textbf{break}\: \mathit{uimm9}}
\\
the \emph{break} instruction causes a debugger trap.
\emph{program counter} and trap cause are saved to privileged
registers for the operating system to dispatch to a debugger
and the \emph{program counter} is set to a trap vector address.

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{j}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00001}{\textbf{j}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 2}
\\
the \emph{j} or \emph{jump} instruction is an unconditional branch instruction
that adds a relative immediate address to the \emph{program counter}. the
resulting \emph{program counter} address is \emph{$[pc + simm9 \times 2]$}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    pc = pc + simm9 * 2
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{b}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00010}{\textbf{b}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 2}
\\
the \emph{b} or \emph{branch} instruction is a conditional branch instruction
that adds a relative immediate address to the \emph{program counter}. if the
\emph{flag} register has been set by a compare instruction, the resulting
\emph{program counter} address is \emph{$[pc + simm9 \times 2]$},
otherwise the \emph{program counter} is advanced normally.
\begin{Verbatim}
    if flag:
        pc = pc + simm9 * 2
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{ibj}
\insnimmnine{simm}{00011}{\textbf{ibj}\: \mathit{simm9} \times 64}
\\
the \emph{ibj} or \emph{immediate-block-jump} instruction adds a
64-bit relative address to the \emph{immediate base} register.
the resulting \emph{immediate base} address is \emph{$[ib + simm9 \times 64]$}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    ib = ib + simm9 * 64
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{link}
\insrcimmsix{fun}{uimm}{00100}{\textbf{link.i64}\: \mathit{fun3}, \mathit{ib64}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{link} instruction loads a 64-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib
+ uimm6 \times 8]$} containing a \emph{i32x2} relative address vector,
which it adds it to \emph{(pc,ib)}, conditionally subtracts the source
link register \emph{(r6 or r7)}, then conditionally adds the difference
to the destination link register \emph{(r6 or r7)}, depending on the
value of \emph{fun3}. the type of linkage in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{value} & \textbf{mnemonic} & \textbf{description} &
\textbf{link-type} & \textbf{dst-link} & \textbf{src-link} \\
\hline
0     & jib      & jump               & jump & -  & -  \\
1     & -        & -                  & -    & -  & -  \\
2     & jalib    & jump-and-link      & call & r6 & -  \\
3     & jalib    & jump-and-link      & call & r7 & -  \\
4     & jtlib    & jump-to-link       & ret  & -  & r6 \\
5     & jtlib    & jump-to-link       & ret  & -  & r7 \\
6     & jalaib   & jump-and-link-add  & tail & r6 & -  \\
7     & jalaib   & jump-and-link-add  & tail & r7 & -  \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Link instruction functions}
\label{tab:link-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\begin{Verbatim}
    lr = 0b110 + (fun3 & 1)
    cval = const-mem<i32x2>[ib + uimm6 * 8]
    dval = fun3 == jalaib ? cval + auth-decrypt(reg<i32x2>[lr]) : cval
    lval = fun3 == jtlib  ?        auth-decrypt(reg<i32x2>[lr]) : 0
    (dpc,dib) = dval
    (lpc,lib) = lval
    pc = pc + dpc - lpc
    ib = ib + dib - lib
    if fun3 == jalib or fun3 == jalaib:
        reg[lr] = auth-encrypt(reg<i32x2>(dpc,dib))
\end{Verbatim}

\par\noindent
the \emph{auth-encrypt} and \emph{auth-decrypt} functions are placeholders
for authenticated encryption, and decryption of relative address vectors.
authentication failures should generate a trap for the operating system to
dispatch to a control flow integrity trap handler.

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movd}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{00101}{\textbf{movd.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{movd} or \emph{move-dword-immediate-block} instruction loads a
32-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$}, which it
sign-extends to 64-bits, then saves to the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movq}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{00110}{\textbf{movq.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib64}(\mathit{uimm6})}
\\
the \emph{movq} or \emph{move-qword-immediate-block} instruction loads a
64-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 8]$} then saves
it to the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = const-mem<i64>[ib + uimm6 * 8]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{movi}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{simm}{00111}{\textbf{movi.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{simm6}}
\\
the \emph{movi} or \emph{move-immediate} instruction sign-extends the
immediate value in \emph{simm6} then saves the result in the \emph{rc}
register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = simm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{addi}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{simm}{01000}{\textbf{addi.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{simm6}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{addi} or \emph{add-immediate} instruction sign-extends the
immediate value in \emph{simm6} then adds it to the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rc] + simm6
    flag = result < reg[rc]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srli}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01001}{\textbf{srli.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{srli} or \emph{shift-right-logical-immediate} instruction
performs a logical right shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. zeros are copied into the left most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<u64>[rc] >> uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srai}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01010}{\textbf{srai.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{srai} or \emph{shift-right-arithmetic-immediate} instruction
performs an arithmetic right shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. the sign is copied into the left most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<i64>[rc] >> uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{slli}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01011}{\textbf{slli.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{uimm6}}
\\
the \emph{slli} or \emph{shift-left-logical-immediate} instruction
performs a logical left shift by \emph{uimm6} bits of the value in
the \emph{rc} register. zeros are copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rc] << uimm6
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{addd}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01100}{\textbf{addd.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6}); \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{addd} or \emph{add-half-immediate-block} instruction loads
a 32-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$}, which it
sign-extends to 64-bits, then adds to the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rc] + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
    flag = result < reg[rc]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{leapc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01101}{\textbf{leapc.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{leapc} or \emph{load-effective-address-pc} instruction
loads a 32-bit constant addressed by \emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$},
which it sign-extends to 64-bits, adds it to the \emph{program counter},
and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{loadpc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01110}{\textbf{loadpc.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{loadpc} instruction loads a 32-bit constant addressed by
\emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$} which it sign-extends to 64-bit, adds it to
the \emph{program counter} to form an address, then loads a 64-bit value
from memory at that address and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = mem<i64>[pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{storepc}
\insrcimmsix{rc}{uimm}{01111}{\textbf{storepc.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{ib32}(\mathit{uimm6})(\mathit{pc})}
\\
the \emph{storepc} instruction loads a 32-bit constant addressed by
\emph{$[ib + uimm6 \times 4]$} which it sign-extends to 64-bit, adds it to the
\emph{program counter} to form an address, then stores to memory at that
address a 64-bit value from the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    mem<i64>[pc + const-mem<i32>[ib + uimm6 * 4]] = reg[rc]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{load}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{uimm}{10000}{\textbf{load.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, (\mathit{uimm3} \times 8)(\mathit{rb})}
\\
the \emph{load} instruction computes the address \emph{$[rb + uimm3 \times 8]$}
then loads a 64-bit value from memory at that address and saves the result in
the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = mem<i64>[reg[rb] + uimm3 * 8]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{store}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{uimm}{10001}{\textbf{store.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, (\mathit{uimm3} \times 8)(\mathit{rb})}
\\
the \emph{store} instruction computes the address \emph{$[rb + uimm3 \times 8]$}
then stores a 64-bit value to memory at that address containing a 64-bit value
from the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    mem<i64>[reg[rb] + uimm3 * 8] = reg[rc]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{compare}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{fun}{10010}{\textbf{cmp.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{fun3}}
\\
the \emph{compare} instruction performs a comparison between the value in
\emph{rb} and \emph{rc} then saves the result in the \emph{flag} register.
the \emph{compare} opcode is also used to perform conditional move whereby
the \emph{rb} register is copied into the \emph{rc} if the \emph{flag}
register is set. the type of comparison in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{value} & \textbf{mnemonic}& \textbf{description} \\
\hline
0     & \emph{lt}    & \emph{less than (signed)}          \\
1     & \emph{ge}    & \emph{greather or equal (signed)}  \\
2     & \emph{eq}    & \emph{equal}                       \\
3     & \emph{ne}    & \emph{not equal}                   \\
4     & \emph{ltu}   & \emph{less than (unsigned)}        \\
5     & \emph{geu}   & \emph{greater or equal (unsigned)} \\
6     & \emph{cmov}  & \emph{conditional move}            \\
7     & \emph{ncmov} & \emph{negated conditional move}    \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Compare instruction functions}
\label{tab:compare-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\begin{Verbatim}
    match fun3
    | lt    -> flag = reg<i64>[rc] <  reg<i64>[rb]
    | ge    -> flag = reg<i64>[rc] >= reg<i64>[rb]
    | eq    -> flag =      reg[rc] =       reg[rb]
    | ne    -> flag =      reg[rc] !=      reg[rb]
    | ltu   -> flag = reg<u64>[rc] <  reg<u64>[rb]
    | geu   -> flag = reg<u64>[rc] >= reg<u64>[rb]
    | cmov  -> if flag:
                   reg[rc] = reg[rb]
    | ncmov -> if not flag:
                   reg[rc] = reg[rb]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{logic}
\insrcrbimmthree{rc}{rb}{fun}{10011}{\textbf{logic.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{fun3}}
\\
the \emph{logic} instruction performs a logic operation on the value
in the \emph{rb} register then stores the result in the \emph{rc}
register. the type of logic operations in \emph{fun3} can be one of:
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{value} & \textbf{mnemonic}& \textbf{description} \\
\hline
0     & \emph{mv}    & \emph{move}                 \\
1     & \emph{not}   & \emph{logical not}          \\
2     & \emph{neg}   & \emph{negate}               \\
3     & \emph{bswap} & \emph{bswap}                \\
4     & \emph{ctz}   & \emph{count trailing zeros} \\
5     & \emph{clz}   & \emph{count leading zeros}  \\
6     & \emph{ctpop} & \emph{count population}     \\
7     & \emph{sext}  & \emph{sign extend}          \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Logic instruction functions}
\label{tab:logic-instruction-functions}
\end{table}

\begin{Verbatim}
    match fun3
    | mov   -> reg[rc] = reg[rb]
    | not   -> reg[rc] = ~reg[rb]
    | neg   -> reg[rc] = -reg[rb]
    | bswap -> reg[rc] = bswap(reg[rb])
    | ctz   -> reg[rc] = ctz(reg[rb])
    | clz   -> reg[rc] = clz(reg[rb])
    | ctpop -> reg[rc] = ctpop(reg[rb])
    | sext  -> reg[rc] = sext(reg[rb])
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{pin}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10100}{\textbf{pin.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{pin} or \emph{pack-indirect} instruction packs two absolute
addresses as an \emph{i32x2} \emph{(pc,ib)} relative address vector.
the register \emph{ra} is subtracted from the \emph{program counter},
and the register \emph{rb} is subtracted from the \emph{immediate base}
register, and the results are packed into an \emph{i32x2} relative address
vector and saved to the register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    lpc   = int<i32>(pc - reg[ra])
    lib   = int<i32>(ib - reg[rb])
    lval  = (lpc,lib)
    reg[rc] = auth-encrypt(lval)
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{and}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10101}{\textbf{and.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{and} instruction performs a \emph{logical-and} of the register
\emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the result in the
register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] & reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{or}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10110}{\textbf{or.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{or} instruction performs a \emph{logical-or} of the register
\emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the result in the
register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] | reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{xor}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{10111}{\textbf{xor.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{xor} instruction performs a \emph{logical-exclusive-or}
of the register \emph{rb} and the register \emph{ra} and saves the
result in the register \emph{rc}.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] ^ reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{add}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11000}{\textbf{add.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{add} instruction adds the \emph{rb} register to
the \emph{ra} register and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register,
and carry is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rb] + reg[ra]
    flag = result < reg[rb]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{srl}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11001}{\textbf{srl.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{srl} or \emph{shift-right-logical} instruction performs a
logical right shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register.
zeros are copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<u64>[rb] >> reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sra}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11010}{\textbf{sra.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{sra} or \emph{shift-right-arithmetic} instruction performs an
arithmetic right shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register. sign is copied into the right most bits.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg<i64>[rb] >> reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sll}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11011}{\textbf{sll.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{sll} or \emph{shift-left-logical} instruction performs a
logical left shift of the value in the \emph{rb} register by the
number of bits in register \emph{ra} then saves the result in the
\emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] << reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{sub}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11100}{\textbf{sub.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}; \mathit{flag}}
\\
the \emph{sub} instruction subtracts the \emph{ra} register from
the \emph{rb} register and saves the result in the \emph{rc} register,
and carry \emph{not borrow} is saved to the \emph{flag} register.
\textbf{note:} this differs from borrow semantics; flag is \emph{true carry}
and can be used with \emph{adc} or \emph{sbc} instructions without inversion.
\begin{Verbatim}
    result = reg[rb] - reg[ra]
    flag = reg[rb] >= reg[ra]
    reg[rc] = result
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{mul}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11101}{\textbf{mul.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{mul} instruction performs signed multiplication of the
\emph{rb} register with the \emph{ra} register and saves the
result in the \emph{rc} register.
\begin{Verbatim}
    reg[rc] = reg[rb] * reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{div}
\insrcrbrc{rc}{rb}{ra}{11110}{\textbf{div.i64}\: \mathit{rc}, \mathit{rb}, \mathit{ra}}
\\
the \emph{div} instruction performs signed division of the
\emph{rb} register by the \emph{ra} register and saves the
result in the \emph{rc} register. division by zero causes
the flag to be set and zero to be stored in the \emph{rc} register.
a subsequent branch can handle the division by zero.
\begin{Verbatim}
    flag = reg[ra] == 0
    if flag:
        reg[rc] = 0
    else:
        reg[rc] = reg[rb] / reg[ra]
\end{Verbatim}

\needspace{8\baselineskip}
\subsection{illegal}
\insnimmnine{uimm}{11111}{\textbf{illegal}\: \mathit{uimm9}}
\\
the \emph{illegal} instruction causes an illegal instruction trap.
\emph{program counter} and trap cause are saved to privileged registers
for the operating system to dispatch to an illegal instruction handler
and the \emph{program counter} is set to a trap vector address.

\chapter{Assembly}

\section{Introduction}

this glyph assembly language reference begins with an introduction to
assembler and linker concepts and terminology, followed by sections
describing the glyph assembler directives, and pseudo-instruction aliases.
section~\ref{sec:instructions} contains a complete listing of instructions.

\section{Concepts}
this section covers the concepts required to understand assembling and
linking executable code from source files. the terminology used in this
section is generally applicable to ELF, PE/COFF, and Mach-O formats, with
the exception that all code is position-independent.

\subsection{assembly}
assembly files contain assembly language directives, macros and
instructions describing program code and data. they can be handwritten
or emitted by a compiler. an assembly file is the input file to the
assembler and the output from the assembler is an object file.

\subsection{object}
object files contain compiled position-independent object code and data with
relocations. an object file cannot be run; rather it is used as input
to the archiver or linker in archiving or linking steps that combine them
to produce an archive or an executable file or library.

\subsection{archive}
archive files contain a collection of compiled position-independent object
files with relocations and are sometimes referred to as static libraries.
archive files use a simple format that concatenates a set of object
files with an index listing the objects contained in the archive.
thin archives contain the index listing the object files without
the associated data.

\subsection{executable}
executable files contain compiled position-independent object code and
data that has been linked together by a linker in a linking step
using multiple object files, archive files, and libraries as input.
executable files can be statically or dynamically linked. dynamically
linked executables express dependencies on libraries which are
loaded at runtime.

\subsection{library}
library files are runtime-loadable archive files containing position-%
independent object code and data, together with a symbol table and metadata.
they are produced by a linker from multiple object files, archive files, and
libraries as input. libraries can be loaded by a runtime loader, and they
express dependencies on other libraries which need to be loaded at runtime.

\subsection{section}
a section is a name for a region of code or data in an object file,
executable file or library file. the files can be made up of
multiple sections where each section corresponds to one specific type of
executable code or data. this list contains the most common types:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \texttt{.text} is a read-only section containing executable code
\item \texttt{.const} is a read-only section containing immediate blocks
\item \texttt{.data} is a read-write section containing global or static variables
\item \texttt{.rodata} is a read-only section containing read-only variables
\item \texttt{.bss} is a read-write section containing uninitialized data
\end{itemize}

\subsection{segment}
segments are loadable regions of code or data in an executable or shared
library. segments describe virtual addresses, file offsets and memory access
permissions for mapped sections. in ELF, a segment can map to one or more
sections. in PE/COFF sections are mapped directly. in Mach-O, sections are
contained within a small set of specific segment types.

\subsection{symbol}
symbols are entries in a metadata table that contain a name with a
mapping to an address. symbols are present in object files and dynamic
symbols are present in libraries and executables. they can be
referred to in relocation entries and debugging metadata.

\subsection{relocation}
relocations are metadata table entries used to update relocatable addresses
during linking. relocations contain a type, a file offset pointing to text
or data, and a pointer to a symbol name whose address needs to be updated
during the linking step. relocations can be present in object files and
dynamic relocations are present in libraries and executables.

\subsection{linking}
linking is the process of combining multiple relocatable object
files by merging and aligning sections, resolving symbol references across
files, assigning final symbol addresses, applying relocation fix-ups using
relocation entries, and adding debug metadata.

\newpage
\section{Directives}
the assembler implements a number of directives that control the assembly
of instructions into object files. these directives are based on the
AT\&T System V assembler~\cite{att-svr4-asm} and the GNU assembler%
~\cite{gnu-as-manual}, with some additions. they provide the ability to
include arbitrary data, align data, export symbols, switch sections,
define constants, and emit metadata.
\\
\par\noindent
the following table lists glyph assembler directives:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Directive} & \textbf{Arguments} & \textbf{Description} \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{Data directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.byte}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 8-bit comma separated words   \\
\texttt{.short}  & \emph{expression-list}    & 16-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.long}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 32-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.quad}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 64-bit comma separated words  \\
\texttt{.octa}   & \emph{expression-list}    & 128-bit comma separated words \\
\texttt{.string} & \emph{“string”}           & emit string                   \\
\texttt{.zero}   & \emph{integer}            & emit zeroes                   \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{Alignment directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.align}  & \emph{pow2 [,pad\_val=0] [,max]} & align to power of 2    \\
\texttt{.balign} & \emph{bytes [,pad\_val=0]}       & byte align             \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{Symbol directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.globl}  & \emph{symbol\_name,const\_name}  & emit symbol \emph{(global scope)}  \\
\texttt{.local}  & \emph{symbol\_name,const\_name}  & emit symbol \emph{(local scope)}   \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{Section directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.text}    &         & emit \texttt{.text} section or make current    \\
\texttt{.const}   &         & emit \texttt{.const} section or make current   \\
\texttt{.data}    &         & emit \texttt{.data} section or make current    \\
\texttt{.rodata}  &         & emit \texttt{.rodata} section or make current  \\
\texttt{.bss}     &         & emit \texttt{.bss} section or make current     \\
\texttt{.common}  & \emph{symbol\_name,size,align} & emit common object to
\texttt{.bss} section \\
\texttt{.section} & \emph{section\_name}    & emit section (default
\texttt{.text}) or make current \\
\hline
\multicolumn{3}{|l|}{\textbf{Miscellaneous directives}} \\ \hline
\texttt{.equ}    & \emph{name, value}       & constant definition            \\
\texttt{.file}   & \emph{“filename”}        & emit filename symbol           \\
\texttt{.ident}  & \emph{“string”}          & emit identification string     \\
\texttt{.size}   & \emph{symbol,symbol}     & emit symbol size               \\
\texttt{.type}   & \emph{symbol,@function}  & emit symbol type               \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Assembler directives}
\label{tab:assembler-directives}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Pseudo-instructions}

the assembler implements a number of convenience pseudo-instruction aliases
that are formed from regular instructions, but have implicit or deduced arguments.
\\
\par\noindent
the following table lists glyph assembler pseudo-instruction aliases:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{Pseudo-instruction} & \textbf{Expansion} & \textbf{Description}   \\
\texttt{nop}   & \texttt{or.i64 r0,r0,r0} & no-operation                     \\
\texttt{li} \emph{rc, expression} & (several expansions) & load immediate  \\
\texttt{la} \emph{rc, symbol}     & (several expansions) & load address    \\
\texttt{call} \emph{symbol}     & \texttt{jalib.i64} \emph{ibcall(text-label,const-label)} & procedure call \\
\texttt{ret}                    & \texttt{jtlib.i64} \emph{ibret(text-label,const-label)} & procedure return \\
\texttt{jib.i64} \emph{ib64(uimm6)} & \texttt{link.i64} \emph{ib64(uimm6),} \texttt{jib}
 & jump-immmediate-block \\
\texttt{jalib.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6)} & \texttt{link.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6),} \texttt{jalib}
 & jump-and-link-immmediate-block \\
\texttt{jtlib.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6)} & \texttt{link.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6),} \texttt{jtlib}
 & jump-to-link-immmediate-block \\
\texttt{jalaib.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6)} & \texttt{link.i64} \emph{rc, ib64(uimm6),} \texttt{jalaib}
 & jump-and-link-add-immmediate-block \\
\texttt{cmp.lt.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{lt} & compare less than (signed) \\
\texttt{cmp.gt.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rb, rc,}
\texttt{lt} & compare greater than (signed) \\
\texttt{cmp.le.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rb, rc,}
\texttt{ge} & compare less or equal (signed) \\
\texttt{cmp.ge.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ge} & compare greater or equal (signed) \\
\texttt{cmp.eq.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{eq} & compare equal \\
\texttt{cmp.ne.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ne} & compare not equal \\
\texttt{cmp.ltu.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ltu} & compare less than (unsigned) \\
\texttt{cmp.gtu.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rb, rc,}
\texttt{ltu} & compare greater than (unsigned) \\
\texttt{cmp.leu.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rb, rc,}
\texttt{geu} & compare less or equal (unsigned) \\
\texttt{cmp.geu.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{geu} & compare greater or equal (unsigned) \\
\texttt{cmov.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{cmov} & conditional move \\
\texttt{ncmov.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{compare.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ncmov} & negated conditional move \\
\texttt{mov.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{mov} & copy register \\
\texttt{not.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{not} & logical not \\
\texttt{neg.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{neg} & signed negate \\
\texttt{bswap.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{bswap} & byte swap \\
\texttt{ctz.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ctz} & count trailing zeros \\
\texttt{clz.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{clz} & count leading zeroes \\
\texttt{ctpop.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{ctpop} & count population \\
\texttt{sext.i64} \emph{rc, rb} & \texttt{logic.i64} \emph{rc, rb,}
\texttt{sext} & sign extend \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Pseudo instructions}
\label{tab:pseudo-instructions}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Calling conventions}
\subsection{calling convention --- 16-bit}

the 16-bit instruction packet, while intended to be used in conjunction
with the larger opcodes, is designed as a complete subset, so there is
an ABI variant that targets a subset of the instruction set architecture
that only uses the 16-bit opcodes.
\\
\par\noindent
the register assignment for the 16-bit subset was chosen with this rationale:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item  2 blocks of 4 contiguous non-volatile \emph{callee-save} and volatile
\emph{caller-save} registers.
\item  3 special registers, 2 argument registers, 1 temporary register,
and 3 save registers.
\item  3 save registers to avoid excessive spilling around function calls.
\item  1 temporary register to avoid spilling arguments to free a temporary.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the calling convention for the 16-bit subset is as follows:

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=0pt]
\item \emph{immediate base} \texttt{ib} is set by \texttt{call} instructions
and must point to a valid immediate block on function entry. function
symbols are exported with two labels; one in the \texttt{.text} section, and
one in the \texttt{.const} section. \emph{immediate base} must be restored
to the entry value in the function \emph{epilogue} before it can be restored
by \texttt{ret}.
\item \emph{argument registers} \texttt{a0} and \texttt{a1} are used for the
first two arguments, and the remaining arguments are passed on the stack.
\emph{return value} is places in \texttt{a0} and \texttt{a1},
\emph{temporary register} \texttt{t0} is a volatile register, and
\emph{frame pointer} (if enabled) uses \texttt{s0}. there are two more
non-volatile \emph{callee-save} registers, \texttt{s1} and \texttt{s2}.
\end{itemize}

\par\noindent
the following table outlines the 16-bit register allocation showing
register name alias, description, and non-volatile \emph{callee-save} or
volatile \emph{caller-save} status.
\\
\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{|l|l|l|l|l|}
\hline
\textbf{name} & \textbf{alias} & \textbf{description} & \textbf{save} \\
\hline
ib    &       & immediate base                              & callee \\
r0    & sp    & stack pointer                               & callee \\
r1    & s0/fp & saved register 0 / frame pointer            & callee \\
r2    & s1    & saved register 1                            & callee \\
r3    & s2    & saved register 2                            & callee \\
r4    & a0    & argument register 0                         & caller \\
r5    & a1    & argument register 1                         & caller \\
r6    & t0    & temporary register 0                        & caller \\
r7    & ra    & return address / (pc,ib) link vector        & caller \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{16-bit register assignment}
\label{tab:register-allocation-16}
\end{table}

\chapter{Appendix}

\section{Opcode summary --- 16-bit}

\newcommand{\ainsnimmnine}[4]{
\draw [line width=0.2mm, black!80] (0,0-#1)    rectangle (9,1-#1);
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (4.5,0.25-#1)  {\textbf{#2}};

\draw [line width=0.2mm, black!80] (9,0-#1)     rectangle (14,1-#1);
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (11.5,0.25-#1)  {\textbf{#3}};

\draw [line width=0.2mm, black!80] (14,0-#1)     rectangle (16,1-#1);
\node [font=\small,anchor=base] at (15.0,0.25-#1)  {\textbf{00}};

\node [font=\small,anchor=base west] at (17.0,0.25-#1)  {$#4$};
}

\newcommand{\ainsrcimmsix}[5]{
\draw [line width=0.2mm, black!80] (0,0-#1)    rectangle (3,1-#1);
